4. **(Dinkvraag) Waarom is dit so 'n groot probleem dat kos verspil word terwyl mense honger ly?** Omdat kos te duur is. / **Omdat daar eintlik genoeg kos is, maar dit bereik nie almal wat dit nodig het nie. ✓** / Omdat mense nie van groente hou nie. / Omdat winkels te klein is. / Omdat plase te ver is.
5. **(Dinkvraag) Wat is die hoofboodskap van die leesstuk?** Winkels moet minder kos verkoop. / Ons moet net rys eet. / Boere moet minder kos kweek. / Oorskietkos is gevaarlik. / **Elke gesin kan help om minder kos te mors. ✓**

### Woordherkenning (10)

toebroodjie, verlep, navorsers, geproduseer, fabrieke, brandstof, stortingsterreine, inkopielys, etikette, liefdadigheidsorganisasies

### Grammatika: die lydende vorm (5)

*Skryf die sin in die lydende vorm – Bv. Die by maak heuning. – Heuning word deur die by gemaak.*

1. Die boer kweek die groente. – **Die groente word deur die boer gekweek.**
2. Die winkel verkoop brood. – **Brood word deur die winkel verkoop.**
3. Ma skryf die inkopielys. – **Die inkopielys word deur Ma geskryf.**
4. Die skool skenk die kos. – **Die kos word deur die skool geskenk.**
5. Ons eet die oorskietkos. – **Die oorskietkos word deur ons geëet.**

### Woordeskat (5)

1. Die slaaiblare het in die warm kombuis \_\_\_\_\_\_. **verlep ✓** / verlaat / verloop / verlepte
2. 'n \_\_\_\_\_\_ het die gewoontes van pikkewyne bestudeer. Navorsing / **Navorser ✓** / Naaldwerker / Navigator
3. Hierdie plaas \_\_\_\_\_\_ elke jaar tonne appels. produk / produksie / **produseer ✓** / prosedeer
4. Lees die \_\_\_\_\_\_ om te sien wanneer die melk verval. etiek / etui / etalage / **etiket ✓**
5. Die \_\_\_\_\_\_ deel warm kos aan haweloses uit. liefdesbrief / liefhebber / lieflikheid / **liefdadigheidsorganisasie ✓**

## Level 5 – Afrikaans 6: Wie is die baas: jy of jou skerm?

Topic: My body and health. About 333 words, 22 sentences, 5 paragraphs, informative and persuasive.

### Woordkaarte (6)

**spandeer** – Om tyd of geld aan iets te gebruik. Bv. Ek spandeer my sakgeld aan boeke. Ek spandeer, ek het gespandeer. Engels: "spend".

**beïnvloed** – Om 'n verandering in iemand of iets te bring. Bv. Die weer beïnvloed my bui. Ek beïnvloed, ek het beïnvloed. Engels: "influence / affect".

**ontwerp** – Om 'n plan of tekening te maak van hoe iets gebou of gemaak moet word. Bv. Sy het haar eie rok ontwerp. Ek ontwerp, ek het ontwerp. Engels: "design".

**versteur** – Om iets te onderbreek of deurmekaar te maak. Bv. Die blaffende hond het my slaap versteur. Ek versteur, ek het versteur. Engels: "disturb".

**verban** – Om iets heeltemal te verbied of weg te stuur. Bv. Die skool het selfone in die klas verban. Ek verban, ek het verban. Engels: "ban".

**toestel** – 'n Masjien of instrument, soos 'n foon of tablet. Bv. Laai jou toestel voor jy gaan slaap. Een toestel, baie toestelle. Engels: "device".

*Extra cards for more support:* dopamien, geïrriteerd

### Die leesstuk

Baie kinders en tieners spandeer deesdae ure elke dag voor 'n skerm. Hulle kyk video's, speel speletjies, gesels met vriende en doen selfs huiswerk op fone, tablette en rekenaars. Skerms is nie sleg nie; hulle kan ons help om te leer en met ander mense te verbind. Maar wetenskaplikes het uitgevind dat te veel skermtyd ons brein en liggaam op onverwagte maniere kan beïnvloed.

Baie speletjies en sosiale media is so ontwerp dat ons wil aanhou kyk. Elke keer wat ons 'n vlak klaarmaak of 'n "like" kry, stel ons brein 'n chemiese stof genaamd dopamien vry. Dit laat ons goed voel, sodat ons nog meer daarvan wil hê. Daarom is dit so moeilik om die foon neer te sit, selfs wanneer ons weet ons moet.

Te veel skermtyd, veral saans, kan ook ons slaap versteur. Die blou lig van skerms laat ons brein dink dat dit nog dag is. Wanneer ons te min slaap, sukkel ons om te konsentreer, raak ons gou geïrriteerd en onthou ons minder van wat ons geleer het. Om ure lank stil te sit, beteken ook dat ons minder beweeg, wat sleg is vir ons spiere, hart en oë.

Die oplossing is nie om skerms heeltemal te verban nie, maar om slim keuses te maak. Stel saam met jou ouers 'n skermplan op, met vaste tye vir skerms en vaste tye daarsonder. Hou fone uit die slaapkamer uit en skakel skerms minstens 'n uur voor slaaptyd af. Volg die 20-20-20-reël: elke twintig minute kyk jy vir twintig sekondes na iets wat omtrent ses meter ver is. Dit gee jou oë 'n kans om te rus.

Gebruik die tyd wat jy wen om te lees, buite te speel, 'n instrument te leer of met jou familie te gesels. Jy sal dalk agterkom dat jy beter slaap, meer energie het en gelukkiger voel. Skerms is wonderlike gereedskap, maar hulle moet jou dien, nie jou beheer nie. Jy is die baas van jou tyd, nie jou toestel nie. Die keuse is joune.

### Begrip (5 vrae, ✓ = reg)

1. **Watter chemiese stof stel die brein vry wanneer ons 'n "like" kry?** Suurstof. / **Dopamien. ✓** / Kalsium. / Suiker. / Kafeïen.
2. **Waarom kan skerms saans ons slaap versteur?** Omdat hulle te hard is. / Omdat hulle warm word. / **Omdat die blou lig ons brein laat dink dat dit nog dag is. ✓** / Omdat hulle ons honger maak. / Omdat hulle te duur is.
3. **Wat is die 20-20-20-reël?** Twintig minute speel, twintig minute rus. / Twintig video's per dag. / Twintig minute lank lees. / Twintig sekondes lank die oë toemaak. / **Elke twintig minute kyk jy twintig sekondes lank na iets wat omtrent ses meter ver is. ✓**
4. **(Dinkvraag) Waarom is dit so moeilik om die foon neer te sit?** **Omdat speletjies en sosiale media ontwerp is om ons goed te laat voel sodat ons wil aanhou. ✓** / Omdat fone swaar is. / Omdat ons ouers dit verbied. / Omdat die battery pap is. / Omdat daar niks anders is om te doen nie.
5. **(Dinkvraag) Wat bedoel die skrywer met "hulle moet jou dien, nie jou beheer nie"?** Skerms moet gratis wees. / Ons moet nooit skerms gebruik nie. / Skerms moet ons huiswerk doen. / **Jy moet self besluit wanneer en hoe lank jy skerms gebruik. ✓** / Skerms moet ons bedien by 'n restaurant.

### Woordherkenning (10)

skerm, rekenaars, wetenskaplikes, beïnvloed, ontwerp, dopamien, versteur, geïrriteerd, slaapkamer, toestel

### Grammatika: sinne verbind met "want" (5)

*Verbind die sinne met "want" – Bv. Ek bly binne. Dit reën. – Ek bly binne, want dit reën.* (With "want", the word order stays the same, unlike "omdat".)

1. Ek skakel my foon af. Ek wil goed slaap. – **Ek skakel my foon af, want ek wil goed slaap.**
2. Sy lees 'n boek. Sy is moeg vir skerms. – **Sy lees 'n boek, want sy is moeg vir skerms.**
3. Ons speel buite. Dit is lekker weer. – **Ons speel buite, want dit is lekker weer.**
4. Hy rus sy oë. Hulle is moeg. – **Hy rus sy oë, want hulle is moeg.**
5. Ek gesels met Ma. Ek het haar gemis. – **Ek gesels met Ma, want ek het haar gemis.**

### Woordeskat (5)

1. Ek \_\_\_\_\_\_ my sakgeld aan boeke. **spandeer ✓** / spanning / spaander / spaar
2. Die weer \_\_\_\_\_\_ my bui. invloed / **beïnvloed ✓** / beïndruk / beloon
3. Die kunstenaar het 'n nuwe logo \_\_\_\_\_\_. ontwerper / ontwaak / ontwyk / **ontwerp ✓**
4. Die harde musiek het my slaap \_\_\_\_\_\_. versterk / **versteur ✓** / verstuur / versteuring
5. Laai jou \_\_\_\_\_\_ voor jy gaan slaap. toestand / toetsie / **toestel ✓** / toesig

## Level 5 – Afrikaans 7: Krag uit die son

Topic: How things work. About 334 words, 24 sentences, 5 paragraphs, informative.

### Woordkaarte (6)

**installeer** – Om 'n toestel of stelsel op sy plek te sit sodat dit kan werk. Bv. Die tegnikus het die nuwe geyser geïnstalleer. Ek installeer, ek het geïnstalleer. Engels: "install".

**sel** – Een van die klein eenhede waaruit iets bestaan, soos 'n sonpaneel of 'n lewende liggaam. Bv. Ons liggaam bestaan uit miljoene selle. Een sel, baie selle. Engels: "cell".

**elektron** – 'n Baie klein deeltjie wat elektrisiteit dra. Bv. Elektrone vloei deur die koperdraad. Een elektron, baie elektrone. Engels: "electron".

**omsetter** – 'n Toestel wat een soort elektriese stroom in 'n ander soort verander. Bv. Die omsetter laat die rekenaar op sonkrag werk. Een omsetter, baie omsetters. Engels: "inverter".

**hernubaar** – Iets wat nie opraak nie, omdat dit weer en weer aangevul word. Bv. Wind en son is hernubare energiebronne. Engels: "renewable".

**besoedel** – Om lug, water of grond vuil en skadelik te maak. Bv. Fabrieke wat rook uitblaas, besoedel die lug. Ek besoedel, ek het besoedel. Engels: "pollute".

*Extra cards for more support:* gelykstroom, wisselstroom

### Die leesstuk

Tydens beurtkrag het baie Suid-Afrikaanse gesinne sonpanele op hul dakke laat installeer. Jy het dalk al die blink, donkerblou panele op huise, skole en selfs by verkeersligte gesien. Maar hoe verander 'n plat stuk glas sonlig in elektrisiteit wat 'n yskas kan laat werk?

'n Sonpaneel bestaan uit baie klein eenhede wat selle genoem word. Hierdie selle is gemaak van silikon, 'n stof wat van sand verkry word. Wanneer sonlig op die silikon val, stamp die lig klein deeltjies, genaamd elektrone, los. Die elektrone begin in een rigting deur drade beweeg, en daardie vloei van elektrone is elektrisiteit. Dit gebeur sonder enige bewegende dele, rook of geraas.

Die elektrisiteit wat 'n sonpaneel maak, word gelykstroom genoem. Die meeste toestelle in ons huise werk egter met wisselstroom. 'n Toestel wat 'n omsetter genoem word, verander die een soort stroom in die ander. Omdat die son nie snags skyn nie, stoor baie mense die ekstra krag van die dag in groot batterye. Dan kan hulle steeds die ligte aanskakel wanneer dit donker is.

Suid-Afrika is een van die sonnigste lande ter wêreld, wat ons 'n uitstekende plek vir sonkrag maak. Sonkrag is skoon, want dit besoedel nie die lug soos steenkoolkragstasies nie. Dit is ook hernubaar, wat beteken dat dit nooit opraak nie, solank die son skyn. Die nadeel is dat die panele en batterye duur is om te koop, al kos die sonlig self niks. Panele werk ook minder goed op bewolkte dae en moet gereeld skoongemaak word, want stof keer die lig.

Ingenieurs werk voortdurend aan beter en goedkoper panele. Party wetenskaplikes ontwikkel selfs panele wat soos gewone dakteëls lyk, of vensters wat elektrisiteit kan maak. Miskien sal elke gebou eendag sy eie klein kragstasie wees. Die volgende keer wat jy in die son staan, onthou dat daardie warm strale ontsaglik baie energie dra. Die son is dalk die grootste kragbron wat ons het. En die beste van alles? Hy stuur nooit vir ons 'n rekening nie!

### Begrip (5 vrae, ✓ = reg)

1. **Waarvan is sonselle gemaak?** Van plastiek. / Van koper. / **Van silikon, wat van sand verkry word. ✓** / Van hout. / Van steenkool.
2. **Wat is elektrisiteit volgens die leesstuk?** Warm lug. / Sonlig. / **'n Vloei van elektrone. ✓** / Rook uit 'n kragstasie. / Water in 'n pyp.
3. **Wat doen 'n omsetter?** Dit maak die panele skoon. / Dit stoor krag. / Dit skakel die ligte aan. / **Dit verander gelykstroom in wisselstroom. ✓** / Dit draai die panele na die son.
4. **(Dinkvraag) Waarom stoor mense die ekstra krag in batterye?** Omdat batterye mooi lyk. / Omdat die panele te warm word. / **Omdat die son nie snags skyn nie. ✓** / Omdat Eskom dit vra. / Omdat die panele anders breek.
5. **(Dinkvraag) Wat bedoel die skrywer met "Hy stuur nooit vir ons 'n rekening nie"?** Die son is lui. / Die posbode bring nie rekeninge nie. / **Sonlig self is gratis. ✓** / Sonpanele is gratis. / Niemand betaal vir krag nie.

### Woordherkenning (10)

beurtkrag, sonpanele, installeer, elektrisiteit, silikon, elektrone, omsetter, batterye, hernubaar, besoedel

### Grammatika: trappe van vergelyking (5)

*Voltooi die trappe van vergelyking – Bv. klein – kleiner – kleinste*

1. goedkoop – **goedkoper** – **goedkoopste**
2. duur – **duurder** – **duurste**
3. sonnig – **sonniger** – **sonnigste**
4. skoon – **skoner** – **skoonste**
5. stil – **stiller** – **stilste**

### Woordeskat (5)

1. Die tegnikus het die sonpanele op ons dak \_\_\_\_\_\_. installeer / **geïnstalleer ✓** / geïnspireer / geïnstrueer
2. 'n Sonpaneel bestaan uit baie klein \_\_\_\_\_\_. sel / seile / **selle ✓** / sele
3. Die \_\_\_\_\_\_ verander gelykstroom in wisselstroom. **omsetter ✓** / omsetting / omsendbrief / omslag
4. Son en wind is \_\_\_\_\_\_ energiebronne. hernubaar / hernude / herkoude / **hernubare ✓**
5. Plastiek in die see \_\_\_\_\_\_ die water. besoek / besorg / **besoedel ✓** / besoedeling

## Level 5 – Afrikaans 8: Die raaisel van die Grosvenor

Topic: Adventure and make-believe (a true story). About 328 words, 23 sentences, 5 paragraphs.

### Woordkaarte (6)

**skeepswrak** – Die oorblyfsels van 'n skip wat gesink of op rotse vasgeloop het. Bv. Duikers het die ou skeepswrak ondersoek. Een skeepswrak, baie skeepswrakke. Engels: "shipwreck".

**vrag** – Die goed wat 'n skip, trok of vliegtuig vervoer. Bv. Die trok se vrag was vol lemoene. Engels: "cargo".

**ongerep** – Nog heeltemal natuurlik, sonder paaie of geboue. Bv. Die berge is nog ongerep. Engels: "unspoilt".

**gerug** – 'n Storie wat mense vir mekaar vertel, al weet niemand of dit waar is nie. Bv. Daar is 'n gerug dat ons 'n nuwe onderwyser kry. Een gerug, baie gerugte. Engels: "rumour".

**legende** – 'n Ou storie wat baie mense glo, al is dit dalk nie heeltemal waar nie. Bv. Die legende vertel van 'n draak in die grot. Een legende, baie legendes. Engels: "legend".

**oordryf** – Om iets groter, beter of erger voor te stel as wat dit werklik is. Bv. Hy oordryf altyd hoe groot die vis was. Ek oordryf, ek het oordryf. Engels: "exaggerate".

*Extra cards for more support:* skattejagter, historikus

### Die leesstuk

Aan die ruwe Wildekus van die Oos-Kaap lê daar 'n geheimsinnige skeepswrak wat al meer as twee eeue lank skattejagters lok. Die Engelse skip, die Grosvenor, het in Augustus 1782 in die nag op die rotse vasgevaar. Dit was op pad van Indië na Engeland, vol passasiers, bemanning en kosbare vrag.

Die meeste mense het die skipbreuk oorleef en die kus bereik. Maar toe begin hul werklike avontuur. Hulle het besluit om te voet na die Kaap te stap, honderde kilometers ver deur ongerepte wildernis. Hulle moes riviere oorsteek, kos soek en deur digte bos baan. Hulle het honger, dors en wilde diere trotseer, en baie het langs die pad omgekom. Maande later het net 'n klein groepie oorlewendes uiteindelik 'n boereplaas bereik.

Nadat die nuus Engeland bereik het, het gerugte begin versprei. Sommige mense het beweer dat die skip goud, silwer en juwele aan boord gehad het. Party het selfs vertel dat 'n beroemde troon vol edelstene in die skip se ruim gelê het, al is daar geen bewys daarvoor nie. Die legende het gegroei, en die Grosvenor het een van die bekendste skatwrakke in Suid-Afrika geword.

Oor die jare het talle skattejagters hul geluk probeer. Een groep het in die vroeë 1900's selfs 'n tonnel begin grawe om van die land af by die wrak uit te kom. Ander het met duikpakke en masjiene die see deursoek. Hier en daar is ou muntstukke, kanonne en ander voorwerpe gevind. Maar die groot skat waarvan almal gedroom het, is nog nooit gevind nie.

Vandag glo historici dat baie van die stories waarskynlik oordryf is. Tog bly die Grosvenor 'n boeiende raaisel. Die ware skat is dalk nie goud nie, maar die verhaal self: van moed, oorlewing en mense wat nie wou opgee nie. Wanneer jy eendag langs die Wildekus stap en die golwe teen die rotse sien breek, dink aan die mense wat daar hul groot avontuur begin het. En wie weet? Miskien lê daar nog iets onder die sand wat wag om ontdek te word.

### Begrip (5 vrae, ✓ = reg)

1. **Wanneer het die Grosvenor op die rotse vasgevaar?** In Januarie 1882. / **In Augustus 1782. ✓** / In Desember 1872. / In Mei 1682. / In 1982.
2. **Waarheen wou die oorlewendes stap?** Na Durban. / Na Engeland. / Na Indië. / **Na die Kaap. ✓** / Na die Oranjerivier.
3. **Watter gerug het oor die skip versprei?** Dat dit deur seerowers gesteel is. / **Dat daar goud, silwer, juwele en selfs 'n troon aan boord was. ✓** / Dat dit nooit gesink het nie. / Dat die kaptein nog lewe. / Dat die skip vol diere was.
4. **(Dinkvraag) Waarom sê die skrywer die ware skat is dalk die verhaal self?** Omdat die goud reeds verkoop is. / Omdat stories geld werd is. / **Omdat die moed en oorlewing van die mense meer werd is as goud. ✓** / Omdat niemand van goud hou nie. / Omdat die wrak in 'n boek is.
5. **(Dinkvraag) Waarom dink historici baie van die stories is oordryf?** Omdat historici nie van stories hou nie. / Omdat die skip nooit bestaan het nie. / Omdat die see te diep is. / **Omdat daar geen bewys is nie en die groot skat nog nooit gevind is nie. ✓** / Omdat die tonnel ineengestort het.

### Woordherkenning (10)

Wildekus, geheimsinnige, skeepswrak, skattejagters, passasiers, bemanning, oorlewendes, gerugte, legende, oordryf

### Grammatika: verlede tyd van moeilike werkwoorde (5)

*Skryf die sin in die verlede tyd – Bv. Ek eet. – Ek het geëet.* (Watch out: "is" becomes "was", and "het" becomes "het … gehad".)

1. Hulle dink dit is 'n skat. – **Hulle het gedink dit is 'n skat.**
2. Die skip bring vrag. – **Die skip het vrag gebring.**
3. Hy weet van die wrak. – **Hy het van die wrak geweet.**
4. Ons is bang vir die storm. – **Ons was bang vir die storm.**
5. Sy het 'n kaart. – **Sy het 'n kaart gehad.**

### Woordeskat (5)

1. Duikers het die ou \_\_\_\_\_\_ ondersoek. skeepsbou / **skeepswrak ✓** / skeepsvaart / skeepsmaat
2. Die skip se \_\_\_\_\_\_ was vol tee en sy. vraag / vlag / vrat / **vrag ✓**
3. Daar is 'n \_\_\_\_\_\_ dat die skool 'n nuwe swembad kry. **gerug ✓** / gereg / gerus / gerugte
4. Die \_\_\_\_\_\_ vertel van 'n draak wat in die berg woon. legio / **legende ✓** / leging / legkaart
5. Hy \_\_\_\_\_\_ altyd hoe groot die vis was wat hy gevang het. oordra / oordeel / oordrewe / **oordryf ✓**

## Level 5 – Afrikaans 9: Plant 'n boom, plant die toekoms

Topic: Nature and outdoors. About 327 words, 24 sentences, 5 paragraphs, persuasive.

### Woordkaarte (6)

**sypaadjie** – Die paadjie langs 'n straat waarop mense loop. Bv. Loop op die sypaadjie, nie in die straat nie. Een sypaadjie, baie sypaadjies. Engels: "pavement".

**fotosintese** – Die proses waardeur plante sonlig, water en koolstofdioksied gebruik om kos te maak en suurstof vry te stel. Bv. Blare het sonlig nodig vir fotosintese. Engels: "photosynthesis".

**besoedeling** – Vuil en skadelike stowwe in die lug, water of grond. Bv. Karre veroorsaak baie besoedeling in die stad. Engels: "pollution".

**stabiel** – Vas en bestendig, sonder groot veranderinge. Bv. Die leer staan stabiel teen die muur. Engels: "stable".

**gestres** – Wanneer jy bekommerd en gespanne voel. Bv. Ek voel gestres voor 'n groot toets. Engels: "stressed".

**voorsien** – Om iemand te gee wat hulle nodig het. Bv. Die boom voorsien skaduwee vir die koeie. Ek voorsien, ek het voorsien. Engels: "provide".

*Extra cards for more support:* wildevy, landbou

### Die leesstuk

Stel jou 'n straat sonder een enkele boom voor. In die somer sou die sypaadjies so warm word dat jy nie kaalvoet daarop kan loop nie. Daar sou geen skaduwee wees om onder te speel nie, en geen voëls wat soggens sing nie. Bome is meer as net mooi; hulle is van die belangrikste lewende dinge op ons planeet.

Eerstens maak bome die lug wat ons inasem, skoner. Deur 'n proses wat fotosintese genoem word, neem hulle koolstofdioksied uit die lug op en stel hulle suurstof vry. Hulle vang ook stof en besoedeling op hul blare vas. 'n Enkele groot boom kan oor die jare baie kilogram koolstof in sy stam, takke en wortels stoor. Daarom help bome om die aarde se klimaat stabiel te hou.

Tweedens is bome goed vir ons gesondheid. Studies het getoon dat mense wat naby bome en groen parke woon, minder gestres voel. In stede kan strate met baie bome etlike grade koeler wees as strate sonder bome. Bome se wortels hou ook die grond vas, sodat dit nie tydens swaar reën wegspoel nie.

Derdens is bome 'n huis vir talle diere. Voëls bou neste in hul takke, bye versamel nektar uit hul blomme, en insekte woon in hul bas. 'n Enkele inheemse boom, soos 'n wildevy, kan honderde verskillende soorte diere van kos voorsien. Wanneer ons bome afkap, verloor al hierdie diere hul huis.

Ongelukkig word daar elke jaar wêreldwyd miljoene bome afgekap vir hout, landbou en geboue. Die goeie nuus is dat elkeen van ons kan help. Plant 'n inheemse boom in jou tuin of by jou skool, soos 'n kiepersol, 'n wildepruim of 'n witstinkhout. Gee dit gereeld water in sy eerste paar jaar, en hou dit dop terwyl dit groei. Suid-Afrika vier elke September Arbor-maand, wat 'n perfekte tyd is om 'n boom te plant. Onthou: die beste tyd om 'n boom te plant, was twintig jaar gelede. Die tweede beste tyd is vandag.

### Begrip (5 vrae, ✓ = reg)

1. **Wat gebeur tydens fotosintese?** Bome verloor hul blare. / **Bome neem koolstofdioksied op en stel suurstof vry. ✓** / Bome drink water uit die rivier. / Bome groei vrugte. / Bome slaap in die winter.
2. **Hoe keer bome dat grond wegspoel?** Hul blare keer die reën. / Hul takke hou die wind weg. / **Hul wortels hou die grond vas. ✓** / Hul bas suig die water op. / Hul vrugte bedek die grond.
3. **In watter maand vier Suid-Afrika Arbor-maand?** Maart. / Junie. / **September. ✓** / Desember. / Januarie.
4. **(Dinkvraag) Waarom kan strate met baie bome koeler wees?** Omdat bome die son afskakel. / Omdat bome water spuit. / **Omdat bome skaduwee gee. ✓** / Omdat daar minder mense is. / Omdat dit daar meer reën.
5. **(Dinkvraag) Wat bedoel die skrywer met "Die tweede beste tyd is vandag"?** Bome groei net vandag. / Ons moet twintig jaar wag. / **Al het ons nie vroeër geplant nie, moet ons nou begin. ✓** / Vandag is Arbor-dag. / Bome is vandag goedkoper.

### Woordherkenning (10)

sypaadjies, skaduwee, fotosintese, koolstofdioksied, suurstof, besoedeling, klimaat, gesondheid, inheemse, wildevy

### Grammatika: naamwoorde met -ing (5)

*Maak 'n selfstandige naamwoord – Bv. verander – verandering*

1. besoedel – **besoedeling**
2. beskerm – **beskerming**
3. versamel – **versameling**
4. oorstroom – **oorstroming**
5. verwag – **verwagting**

### Woordeskat (5)

1. Loop op die \_\_\_\_\_\_, nie in die straat nie. sykamer / **sypaadjie ✓** / syspieël / sydeur
2. Plante gebruik sonlig vir \_\_\_\_\_\_. fotografie / fotokopie / **fotosintese ✓** / fotosessie
3. Karre veroorsaak \_\_\_\_\_\_ in die stad. **besoedeling ✓** / besoedel / besoeking / bestelling
4. Ek voel \_\_\_\_\_\_ voor 'n groot toets. gestreep / gestruikel / gestreel / **gestres ✓**
5. Die ou boom \_\_\_\_\_\_ skaduwee vir die koeie. voorsiening / **voorsien ✓** / voorspel / voorsit

## Level 5 – Afrikaans 10: Hoort diere in 'n dieretuin?

Topic: Animals. About 326 words, 24 sentences, 5 paragraphs, a balanced argument.

### Woordkaarte (6)

**wreed** – Wanneer iemand pyn of lyding veroorsaak sonder om om te gee. Bv. Dit is wreed om 'n hond sonder kos te los. Engels: "cruel".

**argument** – 'n Rede wat jy gee om te wys dat jou mening reg is. Bv. Sy het 'n sterk argument vir langer pouses gehad. Een argument, baie argumente. Engels: "argument".

**gedrag** – Die manier waarop iemand of 'n dier optree. Bv. Die juffrou het ons goeie gedrag geprys. Engels: "behaviour".

**voorstander** – Iemand wat 'n plan of idee ondersteun. Bv. Hy is 'n groot voorstander van fietsry. Een voorstander, baie voorstanders. Teenoorgestelde: teenstander. Engels: "supporter".

**uitsterf** – Wanneer die laaste diere van 'n soort doodgaan en daar geen meer oor is nie. Bv. Die dinosourusse het lank gelede uitgesterf. Engels: "become extinct".

**welsyn** – Hoe gesond, veilig en gelukkig iemand of iets is. Bv. Die boer sorg vir die welsyn van sy diere. Engels: "wellbeing".

*Extra cards for more support:* teelprogram, bewaring

### Die leesstuk

Is 'n uitstappie na die dieretuin 'n pretdag, of is dit wreed teenoor die diere? Hierdie vraag laat mense al jare lank stry. Daar is sterk argumente aan albei kante, en om 'n goeie mening te vorm, moet ons na albei luister.

Mense wat teen dieretuine is, sê dat wilde diere vry hoort te wees. In die natuur swerf 'n leeu of olifant kilometers ver op 'n dag, maar in 'n hok het dit net 'n klein stukkie grond. Party diere in swak dieretuine raak verveeld en begin vreemde gedrag toon, soos om heeltyd heen en weer te stap. Kritici glo ook dat mense nie diere net vir ons vermaak moet aanhou nie.

Aan die ander kant sê voorstanders dat goeie dieretuine 'n belangrike rol speel. Baie diere, soos die rooi panda en die Siberiese tier, is so bedreig dat hulle dalk sou uitsterf sonder teelprogramme in dieretuine. Party spesies wat heeltemal uit die natuur verdwyn het, is danksy dieretuine weer in die wild vrygelaat. Dieretuine doen ook navorsing oor siektes en help om beseerde wilde diere te versorg.

Voorstanders wys ook daarop dat dieretuine kinders leer om diere lief te hê. 'n Kind wat 'n olifant van naby sien, sal dalk eendag help om hulle te beskerm. Moderne dieretuine probeer daarom om groter, meer natuurlike hokke te bou, en om diere se lewens interessant te hou met speletjies en uitdagings.

So, wie is reg? Dalk lê die antwoord iewers in die middel. 'n Swak dieretuin, waar diere in klein, vuil hokke sit, kan beslis wreed wees. 'n Goeie dieretuin, wat bewaring, navorsing en die diere se welsyn eerste stel, kan egter baie goed doen. Die volgende keer wat jy 'n dieretuin besoek, kyk mooi: Is die diere aktief en gesond? Het hulle genoeg ruimte en skuilplek? Leer die dieretuin jou iets oor bewaring? Jou antwoorde sal jou help om jou eie mening te vorm. Dit is immers die belangrikste vaardigheid van almal: om self te dink.

### Begrip (5 vrae, ✓ = reg)

1. **Watter vreemde gedrag toon party diere in swak dieretuine?** Hulle sing. / Hulle eet te veel. / **Hulle stap heeltyd heen en weer. ✓** / Hulle slaap nooit. / Hulle praat met mense.
2. **Hoe help goeie dieretuine bedreigde diere?** Hulle verkoop hulle. / **Hulle het teelprogramme om te keer dat die diere uitsterf. ✓** / Hulle hou hulle in klein hokke. / Hulle laat mense hulle voer. / Hulle stuur hulle na ander lande.
3. **Waarom bou moderne dieretuine groter, meer natuurlike hokke?** Om meer geld te maak. / Om die diere weg te steek. / **Om die diere se lewens beter en interessanter te maak. ✓** / Omdat die diere groter geword het. / Omdat die besoekers dit vra.
4. **(Dinkvraag) Waarom sê die skrywer dat die antwoord dalk "in die middel" lê?** Omdat dieretuine in die middel van stede is. / Omdat niemand omgee nie. / Omdat die skrywer nie kan besluit nie. / **Omdat swak dieretuine wreed kan wees, maar goeie dieretuine baie goed kan doen. ✓** / Omdat alle dieretuine dieselfde is.
5. **(Dinkvraag) Wat is volgens die skrywer die belangrikste vaardigheid?** Om vinnig te lees. / Om diere te versorg. / Om 'n dieretuin te bou. / Om met ander saam te stem. / **Om self te dink. ✓**

### Woordherkenning (10)

dieretuin, argumente, kilometers, verveeld, kritici, voorstanders, uitsterf, teelprogramme, navorsing, welsyn

### Grammatika: feit of mening? (5)

*Skryf F vir feit of M vir mening – Bv. Die son kom in die ooste op. – F*

1. Olifante is die grootste landdiere. – **F**
2. Dieretuine is vervelig. – **M**
3. Die rooi panda is 'n bedreigde dier. – **F**
4. Almal behoort van leeus te hou. – **M**
5. Party dieretuine het teelprogramme. – **F**

### Woordeskat (5)

1. Dit is \_\_\_\_\_\_ om 'n dier sonder water te los. **wreed ✓** / wreedheid / wreek / wraak
2. Sy het 'n sterk \_\_\_\_\_\_ vir 'n langer pouse gehad. argumente / arrangement / **argument ✓** / agent
3. Die juffrou het ons goeie \_\_\_\_\_\_ geprys. gedra / gedrang / gedig / **gedrag ✓**
4. Sonder beskerming kan die renoster \_\_\_\_\_\_. uitstel / **uitsterf ✓** / uitspring / uitskel
5. Die boer sorg goed vir die \_\_\_\_\_\_ van sy diere. welkom / welsprekend / **welsyn ✓** / weldaad

## Level 5 – English: Why Sleep Matters

About 334 words, 19 sentences, 5 paragraphs, informative and advice-style.

### Word cards (6)

**discover** – To find out something that nobody knew before, or that you did not know. e.g. The children discovered a bird's nest in the hedge. I discover, I discovered. Afrikaans: "ontdek".

**repair** – To fix something that is broken or damaged. e.g. Dad repaired the flat tyre on my bicycle. I repair, I repaired. Afrikaans: "herstel / regmaak".

**concentrate** – To think carefully about one thing without letting anything distract you. e.g. It is hard to concentrate when the radio is loud. Afrikaans: "konsentreer".

**routine** – The usual order in which you do things every day. e.g. Brushing my teeth is part of my morning routine. One routine, many routines. Afrikaans: "roetine".

**habit** – Something you do so often that you do it without thinking. e.g. Biting your nails is a bad habit. One habit, many habits. Afrikaans: "gewoonte".

**grumpy** – Bad-tempered and easily annoyed. e.g. My brother is always grumpy when he is hungry. Opposite: cheerful. Afrikaans: "knorrig".

*Extra cards for more support:* librarian, pleasantly

### The passage

Do you ever beg your parents for "just five more minutes" when it is time for bed? Most children would much rather stay up late, watching television or playing games with their brothers and sisters. However, scientists have discovered that sleep is one of the most important things you can do for your body and your brain.

While you are fast asleep, your body is actually very busy. It repairs tired muscles, fights off germs and even helps you grow taller. Your brain sorts through everything you learnt during the day and stores the important parts in your memory, almost like a librarian packing books onto the right shelves. That is why a good night's sleep before a test is far more useful than staying up late to study.

How much sleep do you really need? Doctors say that children between six and twelve years old should get between nine and twelve hours every night. Without enough rest, you may feel grumpy, find it hard to concentrate in class and even get sick more often.

Luckily, there are a few simple habits that can help you fall asleep more easily. Try to go to bed at the same time every night, even on weekends, so that your body gets used to a routine. Switch off phones, tablets and televisions at least an hour before bedtime, because the bright light from screens tricks your brain into thinking it is still daytime. Instead, read a book, take a warm bath or listen to quiet music. Keep your bedroom cool, dark and tidy, and avoid sugary snacks or fizzy drinks late in the evening. Plenty of exercise and fresh air during the day will also make you pleasantly tired by the time the sun goes down.

So the next time you feel like arguing about bedtime, remember what is happening inside your body while you sleep. Sleep is not a waste of time at all. It is your secret weapon for a happy, healthy and successful day!

### Comprehension (5 questions, ✓ = correct)

1. **What does your body do while you sleep?** It stops working completely. / **It repairs tired muscles and fights off germs. ✓** / It gets ready to eat. / It forgets what happened during the day. / It only rests your eyes.
2. **How much sleep should children aged six to twelve get?** Four to six / six to eight / **nine to twelve ✓** / twelve to fourteen / ten to fifteen hours.
3. **Why should screens be switched off before bedtime?** They use too much electricity. / They get too hot. / **Their bright light tricks your brain into thinking it is daytime. ✓** / Parents want to use them. / They are too loud.
4. **(Thinking) Why does the writer compare the brain to a librarian?** Because both work in a quiet place. / **Because the brain sorts information the way a librarian sorts books. ✓** / Because librarians go to bed early. / Because the brain is full of books. / Because reading makes you sleepy.
5. **(Thinking) What is the main message of the passage?** Children should never watch television. / **Getting enough sleep is important for a healthy body and brain. ✓** / Scientists sleep more than children. / Studying late is the best way to pass a test. / Warm baths make you tired.

### Word recognition (10)

discovered, repairs, concentrate, routine, habits, grumpy, scientists, muscles, librarian, pleasantly

### Grammar: contractions (5)

*Write the short form – e.g. do not – don't* (accept both ' and ’)

1. it is – **it's**
2. you will – **you'll**
3. they are – **they're**
4. cannot – **can't**
5. we have – **we've**

### Vocabulary (5)

1. My mom always gets \_\_\_\_\_\_ when she has not had her morning coffee. grumpily / gummy / **grumpy ✓** / grump
2. The mechanic will \_\_\_\_\_\_ our car on Monday. prepare / **repair ✓** / repeat / repairs
3. Please be quiet so that I can \_\_\_\_\_\_ on my homework. concentration / celebrate / consonant / **concentrate ✓**
4. Going for a walk after supper is part of our family \_\_\_\_\_\_. route / **routine ✓** / roulette / routing
5. Scientists have \_\_\_\_\_\_ a new kind of frog in the rainforest. discover / discovery / **discovered ✓** / recovered

## Level 5 – English 2: How Does the Internet Reach You?

Topic: How things work. About 350 words, 21 sentences, 5 paragraphs, informative.

### Word cards (6)

**cable** – A thick wire, or a bundle of wires, that carries electricity or information. e.g. The phone cable runs under the street. One cable, many cables. Afrikaans: "kabel".

**continent** – One of the seven very large areas of land on Earth, such as Africa. e.g. Africa is the second-largest continent. One continent, many continents. Afrikaans: "kontinent".

**optical fibre** – A very thin strand of glass that carries information as light. e.g. Optical fibres can carry huge amounts of data. Afrikaans: "optiese vesel".

**packet** – A small parcel; on the internet, a tiny piece of information sent on its own. e.g. The message was split into packets. One packet, many packets. Afrikaans: "pakkie".

**route** – The way you travel to get from one place to another. e.g. We took a different route to school. One route, many routes. Afrikaans: "roete".

**engineer** – A person who designs and builds machines, roads, bridges or systems. e.g. The engineer designed a new bridge. One engineer, many engineers. Afrikaans: "ingenieur".

*Extra cards for more support:* data centre, anchor

### The passage

Every time you watch a video, send a message or search for homework help, information zooms around the world in less than a second. But have you ever wondered how it actually travels? Most people imagine that the internet floats through the air, but the truth is far more surprising.

Almost all of the world's internet traffic travels through cables lying on the bottom of the ocean. These undersea cables stretch for thousands of kilometres between continents. South Africa is connected to Europe, Asia and the Americas by several of these cables, which come ashore at places like Melkbosstrand and Mtunzini. Inside each cable are hair-thin strands of glass called optical fibres. Information travels through them as flashes of light.

When you send a photo to a friend, your device breaks it into thousands of tiny pieces called packets. Each packet is labelled with the address of where it must go, a little like a letter in the post. The packets may travel along different routes, speeding through cables, cell towers and huge buildings full of computers called data centres. When they arrive, your friend's phone puts the pieces back together in the right order, and the photo appears. All of this usually happens in the blink of an eye.

The system works well, but it is not perfect. Sometimes a ship's anchor or an underwater landslide damages a cable. When that happens, the internet in whole countries can become slow for weeks until special repair ships fix it. Engineers keep building new cables so that there are always other routes if one breaks.

The next time a web page loads in a flash, spare a thought for the long journey that information has made. It may have raced under oceans, past sleeping whales and through deep valleys on the sea floor. The internet may feel like magic, but it is really the result of clever engineering and teamwork between countries. Understanding how it works helps us to appreciate it, and perhaps one day you could help to build the next part of it!

### Comprehension (5 questions, ✓ = correct)

1. **How does most internet traffic travel between continents?** Through the air from satellites only. / **Through cables on the bottom of the ocean. ✓** / By radio from cell towers. / By aeroplane. / Through pipes in the sky.
2. **How does information travel through optical fibres?** As sound. / As electricity only. / As water. / **As flashes of light. ✓** / As tiny letters.
3. **What happens to a photo when you send it?** It is printed and posted. / **It is broken into tiny pieces called packets. ✓** / It is sent in one piece. / It is made smaller by hand. / It is stored in a cable.
4. **(Thinking) Why do engineers keep building new cables?** To make the ocean prettier. / **So that there are other routes if one cable breaks. ✓** / To catch fish. / Because old cables melt. / To give whales something to follow.
5. **(Thinking) What is the writer's main message?** The internet is magic. / Cables are dangerous for whales. / **The internet is the result of clever engineering and teamwork, not magic. ✓** / Phones are better than computers. / We should use the internet less.

### Word recognition (10)

information, travels, surprising, undersea, continents, optical, packets, labelled, engineers, appreciate

### Grammar: the passive voice (5)

*Rewrite in the passive – e.g. The dog chased the cat. – The cat was chased by the dog.*

1. The ship damaged the cable. – **The cable was damaged by the ship.**
2. Engineers build new cables. – **New cables are built by engineers.**
3. My friend sent the photo. – **The photo was sent by my friend.**
4. The phone rebuilt the picture. – **The picture was rebuilt by the phone.**
5. Repair ships fix the cables. – **The cables are fixed by repair ships.**

### Vocabulary (5)

1. Africa is the second-largest \_\_\_\_\_\_. content / **continent ✓** / continents / constant
2. The builders laid a long \_\_\_\_\_\_ under the road. table / label / cables / **cable ✓**
3. We took a different \_\_\_\_\_\_ to avoid the traffic. **route ✓** / root / router / roast
4. An \_\_\_\_\_\_ designed a stronger bridge. engine / engineers / ingenious / **engineer ✓**
5. Your message is split into tiny \_\_\_\_\_\_. pockets / **packets ✓** / puppets / picket

## Level 5 – English 3: The Truth About Sugar

Topic: Food and cooking. About 336 words, 23 sentences, 5 paragraphs, informative and persuasive.

### Word cards (6)

**fibre** – The rough part of plant foods that helps your stomach digest food. e.g. Brown bread has more fibre than white bread. Afrikaans: "vesel".

**slump** – A sudden drop in energy, strength or amount. e.g. After lunch I often feel a slump. Afrikaans: "insinking".

**bacteria** – Tiny living things, too small to see, some of which can cause illness. e.g. Wash your hands to get rid of bacteria. Afrikaans: "bakterieë".

**recommend** – To say that something is good or the right thing to do. e.g. The doctor recommends eating more vegetables. I recommend, I recommended. Afrikaans: "aanbeveel".

**sneaky** – Doing things in a secret or tricky way. e.g. The sneaky cat stole the fish. Afrikaans: "skelm".

**balance** – The right amount of different things, so that no one thing is too much. e.g. A healthy diet has a good balance of foods. Afrikaans: "balans".

*Extra cards for more support:* diabetes, glucose

### The passage

Sweets, fizzy drinks, biscuits and ice cream: most of us love sugary treats. There is nothing wrong with enjoying them now and then. The problem is that sugar is hiding in many foods where we least expect it, and most of us eat far more than our bodies need.

Our bodies do need some sugar, because it gives us energy. However, the natural sugar found in fruit comes packed with fibre, vitamins and water, which slow down how fast it enters the blood. Added sugar, on the other hand, is poured into food during cooking or in factories. It gives a quick burst of energy, often followed by a slump that can leave you tired, grumpy and hungry again.

Too much added sugar can harm your health in several ways. The bacteria in your mouth feed on sugar and make acid that eats holes in your teeth. Eating lots of sugar over many years can also lead to weight gain and illnesses such as type 2 diabetes. Health experts recommend that children eat no more than about six teaspoons of added sugar a day. Yet a single can of fizzy drink can contain around eight!

The good news is that small changes make a big difference. Swap fizzy drinks for water, and try adding slices of lemon or mint for flavour. Choose a piece of fruit instead of a chocolate bar. Learn to read food labels, because sugar has many sneaky names, such as glucose, sucrose and corn syrup. You may be surprised to find it in tomato sauce, bread and even so-called "healthy" breakfast cereals.

Nobody is saying you must never eat a sweet again. Birthday cake and the occasional ice cream are part of life's pleasures. The secret is balance. When sugary treats are just that, treats, your body gets the energy it needs from healthy food, and your teeth stay strong. Next time you reach for a sugary snack, ask yourself: "Is this a treat, or has it become a habit?" Your future self will thank you.

### Comprehension (5 questions, ✓ = correct)

1. **Why is the sugar in fruit better for you than added sugar?** It is sweeter. / **It comes with fibre, vitamins and water, which slow down how fast it enters the blood. ✓** / It has no energy. / It is more expensive. / It is made in factories.
2. **How does sugar damage your teeth?** It makes them yellow. / It makes them loose. / **Bacteria feed on it and make acid that eats holes in teeth. ✓** / It freezes them. / It makes them grow crooked.
3. **About how much added sugar should children eat a day, at most?** Two teaspoons. / **About six teaspoons. ✓** / Twelve teaspoons. / A cup. / As much as they like.
4. **(Thinking) Why does the writer call some of sugar's names "sneaky"?** Because sugar is a thief. / **Because sugar hides under other names on labels, so it is hard to spot. ✓** / Because the names are rude. / Because they are hard to spell. / Because factories are secret.
5. **(Thinking) What does the writer mean by "The secret is balance"?** Never eat sugar at all. / Eat only sweets. / Stand on one leg while eating. / Eat the same amount of every food. / **Enjoy treats now and then, but eat mostly healthy food. ✓**

### Word recognition (10)

sugary, treats, expect, energy, vitamins, bacteria, diabetes, teaspoons, recommend, cereals

### Grammar: joining sentences (5)

*Join the sentences using the word in brackets – e.g. I stayed inside. It was raining. (because) – I stayed inside because it was raining.*

1. I drank water. I was thirsty. (because) – **I drank water because I was thirsty.**
2. Fruit is sweet. It is healthy. (but) – **Fruit is sweet, but it is healthy.**
3. The cake was delicious. I had a second slice. (so) – **The cake was delicious, so I had a second slice.**
4. I brushed my teeth. I went to bed. (before) – **I brushed my teeth before I went to bed.**
5. She ate an apple. She was hungry. (because) – **She ate an apple because she was hungry.**

### Vocabulary (5)

1. Brown bread contains more \_\_\_\_\_\_ than white bread. fire / fever / **fibre ✓** / filter
2. Wash your hands to remove harmful \_\_\_\_\_\_. cafeteria / batteries / **bacteria ✓** / backs
3. The dentist \_\_\_\_\_\_ brushing twice a day. recommend / remembers / recycles / **recommends ✓**
4. A healthy diet needs a good \_\_\_\_\_\_ of foods. **balance ✓** / balloon / ballot / balcony
5. The \_\_\_\_\_\_ fox crept into the henhouse. sneezy / snaky / **sneaky ✓** / sneaker

## Level 5 – English 4: A Memory Like an Elephant

Topic: Animals. About 340 words, 22 sentences, 5 paragraphs, informative.

### Word cards (6)

**remarkable** – Unusual and surprising in a way that makes people notice. e.g. She has a remarkable singing voice. Afrikaans: "merkwaardig".

**matriarch** – The oldest female who leads a family or group. e.g. The matriarch led the herd to the river. Afrikaans: "matriarg".

**recognise** – To know someone or something because you have seen or heard them before. e.g. I recognised my teacher at the shop. I recognise, I recognised. Afrikaans: "herken".

**mourn** – To feel and show great sadness because someone has died. e.g. The town mourned the loss of the old doctor. Afrikaans: "rou".

**relative** – A member of your family. e.g. We visited our relatives at Christmas. One relative, many relatives. Afrikaans: "familielid".

**compliment** – Something nice that you say about someone. e.g. She gave me a compliment on my drawing. One compliment, many compliments. Afrikaans: "kompliment".

*Extra cards for more support:* poaching, researcher

### The passage

People often say that "an elephant never forgets". For a long time, this was just a saying. Today, scientists who study elephants in the wild have discovered that it is surprisingly close to the truth. African elephants have some of the most remarkable memories in the animal kingdom.

An elephant's brain is the largest of any land animal, weighing around five kilograms. Elephants live in family groups called herds, which are led by the oldest and wisest female, known as the matriarch. The matriarch remembers where to find water and food, even in places the herd hasn't visited for many years. During a terrible drought in Tanzania, researchers noticed that herds led by older matriarchs, who remembered an earlier drought, survived much better. Their memories quite literally saved lives.

Elephants also remember other elephants and people. They can recognise the calls of family members they have not seen for years. Studies in Kenya showed that elephants can even tell the difference between people from groups that have threatened them in the past and those who have not. They do this by smell and by the sound of people's voices.

Their memories are closely linked to their feelings. Elephants are known to mourn their dead. They sometimes stand quietly around the body of a family member, gently touching it with their trunks. Years later, some herds pause when they pass the bones of a relative, as if they are remembering. Elephant calves that lose their mothers can carry their sadness for a long time, which is why orphanages give them loving human carers.

Sadly, elephants face many dangers today, from poaching to losing their homes as towns and farms grow. When an old matriarch dies, her herd loses a lifetime of knowledge. This is one more reason why protecting elephants, especially the older ones, is so important. The next time someone says you have "a memory like an elephant", take it as a compliment. After all, it is one of the most powerful minds in the natural world.

### Comprehension (5 questions, ✓ = correct)

1. **Who leads an elephant herd?** The biggest male. / The youngest calf. / **The oldest and wisest female, called the matriarch. ✓** / A ranger. / The fastest runner.
2. **What did researchers notice during the drought in Tanzania?** All the herds moved to Kenya. / **Herds led by older matriarchs survived much better. ✓** / Elephants stopped drinking water. / The young elephants led the herds. / It rained every day.
3. **How can elephants tell different groups of people apart?** By the colour of their clothes. / By their cars. / **By smell and by the sound of their voices. ✓** / By the food they carry. / They can't.
4. **(Thinking) Why does a herd lose so much when an old matriarch dies?** It loses its biggest elephant. / It must find a new waterhole every day. / **It loses her lifetime of knowledge about where to find food and water. ✓** / The herd must split up. / The calves stop growing.
5. **(Thinking) Why should "a memory like an elephant" be taken as a compliment?** Because elephants are big. / Because elephants are grey. / Because elephants never sleep. / Because elephants are funny. / **Because elephants have some of the most powerful memories in nature. ✓**

### Word recognition (10)

remarkable, kingdom, kilograms, matriarch, drought, researchers, recognise, threatened, mourn, compliment

### Grammar: reported speech (5)

*Rewrite in reported speech – e.g. Sam said, "I am tired." – Sam said that he was tired.* (The site should accept small variations.)

1. The scientist said, "Elephants remember." – **The scientist said that elephants remembered.**
2. Lisa said, "I love elephants." – **Lisa said that she loved elephants.**
3. Tom said, "I am hungry." – **Tom said that he was hungry.**
4. The ranger said, "The herd is near." – **The ranger said that the herd was near.**
5. Mom said, "We are going to the park." – **Mom said that they were going to the park.**

### Vocabulary (5)

1. The \_\_\_\_\_\_ led her herd to the waterhole. monarch / **matriarch ✓** / matriarchs / march
2. Yesterday I \_\_\_\_\_\_ my old teacher at the shop. recognise / recommend / reorganised / **recognised ✓**
3. The whole village \_\_\_\_\_\_ the loss of the kind old chief. **mourned ✓** / moaned / mowed / morning
4. We visit all our \_\_\_\_\_\_ in the Eastern Cape every December. relatively / relax / related / **relatives ✓**
5. She paid me a \_\_\_\_\_\_ on my new haircut. complement / **compliment ✓** / complain / completed

## Level 5 – English 5: A Traffic Jam in Space

Topic: Space and stars. About 331 words, 22 sentences, 5 paragraphs, informative and persuasive.

### Word cards (6)

**incredible** – So amazing that it is hard to believe. e.g. The view from the mountain was incredible. Afrikaans: "ongelooflik".

**satellite** – A machine sent into space to travel around the Earth and collect or send information. e.g. Weather satellites take pictures of clouds. One satellite, many satellites. Afrikaans: "satelliet".

**orbit** – The curved path that something travels around a planet or star. e.g. The Moon is in orbit around the Earth. Afrikaans: "wentelbaan".

**collision** – When two things crash into each other. e.g. There was a collision between two cars. One collision, many collisions. Afrikaans: "botsing".

**debris** – Broken pieces left after something has been destroyed. e.g. The storm left debris all over the road. Afrikaans: "brokstukke".

**atmosphere** – The layer of gases around the Earth. e.g. Meteors burn up in the atmosphere. Afrikaans: "atmosfeer".

*Extra cards for more support:* harpoon, spatula

### The passage

When you look up at the night sky, it seems calm and empty. But hundreds of kilometres above your head, a huge traffic jam is growing. Around our planet, millions of pieces of space junk are racing along at incredible speeds.

Space junk is anything people have sent into space that is no longer useful. It includes old satellites that have stopped working, used rocket parts, and even a glove and a spatula that astronauts accidentally let go of! Most pieces are tiny, like flecks of paint, but thousands are bigger than a tennis ball.

The problem is speed. In orbit, space junk can travel at around 28 000 kilometres an hour, many times faster than a bullet. At that speed, even a small bolt can smash through a satellite like a cannonball. The International Space Station sometimes has to move out of the way to avoid dangerous pieces. Scientists worry that one day, collisions could create so much debris that parts of space become too dangerous to use. This chain reaction even has a name: the Kessler syndrome.

Why should we care? Satellites help with weather forecasts, maps on our phones, television and the internet. If they are destroyed, many things we rely on every day could stop working. Engineers are now testing clever ways to clean up space. Some ideas include giant nets, magnetic arms and even harpoons to catch old satellites and pull them down, so that they burn up in the atmosphere.

Countries have also agreed on rules to help. New satellites should be designed to fall back towards Earth and burn up once their jobs are done. Just as we shouldn't litter on the beach, we shouldn't litter in space. Space may be enormous, but the part of it that we use is surprisingly crowded. If we look after it now, future astronauts, scientists and maybe even you will be able to explore it safely. The sky is no longer the limit, but it still needs protecting.

### Comprehension (5 questions, ✓ = correct)

1. **What is space junk?** Rocks from other planets. / Shooting stars. / **Anything people have sent into space that is no longer useful. ✓** / Dust from the Moon. / Rubbish that aliens left behind.
2. **How fast can space junk travel in orbit?** About 100 kilometres an hour. / As fast as a car. / **Around 28 000 kilometres an hour. ✓** / As fast as a bicycle. / It doesn't move.
3. **Why does the International Space Station sometimes move?** To get closer to the Moon. / **To avoid dangerous pieces of space junk. ✓** / To take photos. / To pick up astronauts. / To follow the Sun.
4. **(Thinking) Why should people on Earth care about space junk?** It might fall on their houses every day. / It blocks out the stars. / **It could destroy the satellites we rely on for weather forecasts, maps and the internet. ✓** / It makes the sky dirty. / It is very noisy.
5. **(Thinking) Why does the writer compare space junk to litter on the beach?** Because space has sand. / Because astronauts go to the beach. / **Because both are rubbish we leave behind that harms places we need to keep clean. ✓** / Because the sea is full of satellites. / Because both are blue.

### Word recognition (10)

kilometres, incredible, satellites, astronauts, accidentally, collisions, debris, syndrome, atmosphere, protecting

### Grammar: prefixes that mean "not" (5)

*Add im-, in-, ir- or il- – e.g. possible – impossible*

1. correct – **incorrect**
2. regular – **irregular**
3. legal – **illegal**
4. visible – **invisible**
5. patient – **impatient**

### Vocabulary (5)

1. The view from the top was \_\_\_\_\_\_. incredibly / **incredible ✓** / credible / inedible
2. Weather \_\_\_\_\_\_ take pictures of clouds from space. **satellites ✓** / satellite / settlements / saddles
3. The Moon travels in an \_\_\_\_\_\_ around the Earth. orbits / robot / orchid / **orbit ✓**
4. There was a \_\_\_\_\_\_ between two cars at the corner. collection / **collision ✓** / collisions / collapse
5. Meteors burn up in the Earth's \_\_\_\_\_\_. atmospheric / hemisphere / atom / **atmosphere ✓**

## Level 5 – English 6: Every Child Should Learn to Swim

Topic: Sport and games. About 329 words, 25 sentences, 5 paragraphs, persuasive.

### Word cards (6)

**flock** – To go somewhere in large numbers. e.g. Tourists flock to Cape Town in summer. They flock, they flocked. Afrikaans: "saamdrom".

**drown** – To die because you are under water and cannot breathe. e.g. Never swim alone, or you could drown. Afrikaans: "verdrink".

**supervise** – To watch over someone to make sure they are safe and doing the right thing. e.g. A teacher supervises the children at break. I supervise, I supervised. Afrikaans: "toesig hou".

**joint** – A place in your body where two bones meet, such as your knee or elbow. e.g. My grandma's joints ache in cold weather. One joint, many joints. Afrikaans: "gewrig".

**guarantee** – A promise that something will definitely happen. e.g. There is no guarantee that it will rain. Afrikaans: "waarborg".

**lifeguard** – A person whose job is to watch swimmers and rescue anyone in danger. e.g. The lifeguard blew her whistle. One lifeguard, many lifeguards. Afrikaans: "lewensredder".

*Extra cards for more support:* municipality, snorkel

### The passage

South Africa has beautiful beaches, rivers, dams and thousands of backyard pools. Every summer, families flock to the water to cool off and have fun. Yet every year, too many children drown, often in places they know well, like a pool at home or at a friend's house. In my opinion, learning to swim should be as important as learning to read.

Firstly, swimming saves lives. Drowning is one of the leading causes of accidental death among young children in our country. It is also frighteningly quick and quiet. A child who is drowning usually cannot shout or wave for help. Children who have had proper swimming lessons are far more likely to stay calm, float and reach safety if they fall into the water.

Secondly, swimming is one of the healthiest sports there is. It works almost every muscle in your body, while the water supports your weight and protects your joints. It strengthens your heart and lungs and improves your fitness. Unlike some sports, you can keep swimming your whole life, from toddler to grandparent.

Thirdly, swimming opens the door to many other adventures. If you can swim confidently, you can learn to surf, snorkel, sail or paddle a canoe. You can join a lifesaving club, where you learn to rescue others. Some of the proudest moments in South African sport have happened in the pool, where our swimmers have won Olympic gold medals.

Of course, learning to swim is not a guarantee against danger. Children should always be supervised near water, pools should be fenced, and everyone should swim only in safe areas with lifeguards. But lessons are one of the most powerful tools we have. Some municipalities and clubs offer free or low-cost lessons in summer. So, if you cannot swim yet, ask a parent or teacher to help you find a class. It could be the most important thing you ever learn. And it might just be the most fun as well!

### Comprehension (5 questions, ✓ = correct)

1. **Where do children often drown, according to the writer?** Only in the sea. / Only in rivers. / **In places they know well, like a pool at home or at a friend's house. ✓** / In the bath. / In school pools.
2. **Why is drowning so dangerous?** It is always loud. / **It is quick and quiet, and a drowning child usually cannot shout for help. ✓** / It only happens at night. / It happens slowly. / It only happens to adults.
3. **Why is swimming good for your joints?** Because the water is cold. / Because you move slowly. / **Because the water supports your weight. ✓** / Because you wear goggles. / Because you only use your arms.
4. **(Thinking) Why does the writer say lessons are "not a guarantee against danger"?** Because lessons are too expensive. / Because nobody can learn to swim. / **Because even good swimmers still need supervision and safe places to swim. ✓** / Because pools are always dangerous. / Because lifeguards are not real.
5. **(Thinking) What is the writer's opinion?** Swimming is boring. / Only adults should swim. / Pools should be closed. / Reading is more important than swimming. / **Every child should learn to swim. ✓**

### Word recognition (10)

beaches, opinion, accidental, frighteningly, supervised, muscles, strengthens, confidently, guarantee, lifeguards

### Grammar: fact or opinion? (5)

*Write F for fact or O for opinion – e.g. The sun rises in the east. – F*

1. Swimming works almost every muscle in your body. – **F**
2. Swimming is the most fun sport in the world. – **O**
3. South African swimmers have won Olympic gold medals. – **F**
4. Everyone should love the beach. – **O**
5. Summer is the best season of the year. – **O**

### Vocabulary (5)

1. Every December, visitors \_\_\_\_\_\_ to the beaches. flick / **flock ✓** / flocks / block
2. A lifeguard watches the pool so that nobody \_\_\_\_\_\_. drown / frowns / **drowns ✓** / crowns
3. An adult must \_\_\_\_\_\_ children near the water. supervisor / superstar / surprise / **supervise ✓**
4. Swimming is gentle on your \_\_\_\_\_\_. **joints ✓** / points / jokes / juice
5. The \_\_\_\_\_\_ rescued a swimmer from the waves. lifetime / lifestyle / **lifeguard ✓** / landlord

## Level 5 – English 7: The Problem with Plastic

Topic: Nature and outdoors. About 341 words, 26 sentences, 5 paragraphs, informative and persuasive.

### Word cards (6)

**crumble** – To break into small pieces. e.g. The old biscuit crumbled in my hand. It crumbles, it crumbled. Afrikaans: "verkrummel".

**microplastics** – Tiny pieces of plastic, smaller than a grain of rice. e.g. Microplastics have been found inside fish. Afrikaans: "mikroplastiek".

**stormwater drain** – A pipe or channel that carries rainwater away from streets. e.g. Litter in the street washes into the stormwater drains. Afrikaans: "stormwaterafvoer".

**tangled** – Twisted together in a messy way that is hard to undo. e.g. The kite string got tangled in the tree. Afrikaans: "verstrengel".

**reusable** – Able to be used again and again. e.g. I take a reusable bag to the shop. Afrikaans: "herbruikbaar".

**campaign** – A planned series of actions to achieve something, such as changing people's minds. e.g. The class started a campaign to save water. One campaign, many campaigns. Afrikaans: "veldtog".

*Extra cards for more support:* trench, single-use

### The passage

Plastic is everywhere. It wraps our food, holds our drinks, and is used to make toys, clothes and phones. It is cheap, light and strong, which makes it very useful. But the very things that make plastic so useful also make it a serious problem for our planet.

Most plastic takes hundreds of years to break down. Even then, it doesn't disappear. Instead, it crumbles into tiny pieces called microplastics, which are smaller than a grain of rice. Every year, millions of tonnes of plastic end up in the ocean, washed there by rivers, stormwater drains and the wind. Scientists have found microplastics everywhere they have looked, from the deepest ocean trenches to the snow on mountain tops.

Sea animals suffer the most. Turtles mistake floating plastic bags for jellyfish, one of their favourite foods. Seabirds feed bottle caps to their chicks, thinking they are fish. Seals and dolphins get tangled in old fishing nets and cannot swim to the surface to breathe. Along the South African coast, volunteers regularly find animals caught in plastic during beach clean-ups.

The good news is that we can all be part of the solution. The simplest step is to use less plastic in the first place. Carry a reusable water bottle and shopping bag, and say no to plastic straws. When you do use plastic, recycle it properly. Join a beach or river clean-up in your area; even one hour can fill bags with rubbish. You could also encourage your school to start a campaign against single-use plastic.

Some people argue that one person cannot make a difference, because the problem is so enormous. But every piece of plastic you avoid is one less piece that could end up in a turtle's stomach. When millions of people make small changes, big things begin to happen. Several countries, including South Africa, now charge money for plastic shopping bags, and many shops have stopped handing out plastic straws. The ocean gives us food, oxygen and joy. It is time we gave something back.

### Comprehension (5 questions, ✓ = correct)

1. **What are microplastics?** Plastic toys. / **Tiny pieces of plastic, smaller than a grain of rice. ✓** / Plastic bottles. / A type of fish. / Fishing nets.
2. **How does plastic reach the ocean?** Only from ships. / **It is washed there by rivers, stormwater drains and the wind. ✓** / It falls from the sky as rain. / Fish carry it there. / It grows there.
3. **Why do turtles eat plastic bags?** They are very hungry. / They like the taste. / **They mistake them for jellyfish. ✓** / People feed them bags. / They use them to build nests.
4. **(Thinking) How does the writer answer people who say one person can't make a difference?** The writer agrees with them. / **Every piece of plastic avoided matters, and when millions make small changes, big things happen. ✓** / Only governments can help. / The problem is too small to worry about. / People should stop going to the beach.
5. **(Thinking) What does the writer mean by "It is time we gave something back"?** We should give the ocean presents. / We should throw more things into the sea. / We should pay to visit the beach. / **We should look after the ocean, because it gives us so much. ✓** / We should return our plastic bags to the shop.

### Word recognition (10)

plastic, serious, microplastics, stormwater, scientists, trenches, jellyfish, tangled, reusable, campaign

### Grammar: the prefix re- (5)

*Add re- to mean "again" – e.g. play – replay*

1. use – **reuse**
2. cycle – **recycle**
3. fill – **refill**
4. build – **rebuild**
5. write – **rewrite**

### Vocabulary (5)

1. The dry cake began to \_\_\_\_\_\_ when I cut it. **crumble ✓** / crumple / tumble / crumbs
2. Tiny pieces of plastic are called \_\_\_\_\_\_. microphones / **microplastics ✓** / microscopes / microwaves
3. The dolphin was \_\_\_\_\_\_ in an old fishing net. tangle / tingled / tanned / **tangled ✓**
4. Take a \_\_\_\_\_\_ bag when you go shopping. reused / refusable / **reusable ✓** / useless
5. Our school started a \_\_\_\_\_\_ to stop using plastic straws. champagne / campsite / camping / **campaign ✓**

## Level 5 – English 8: The Mystery of the Mary Celeste

Topic: Adventure and make-believe (a true mystery). About 330 words, 25 sentences, 5 paragraphs.

### Word cards (6)

**drift** – To be carried slowly by wind or water. e.g. The empty boat drifted out to sea. It drifts, it drifted. Afrikaans: "dryf".

**puzzle** – To confuse someone because they cannot understand something. e.g. The strange noise puzzled us. It puzzles, it puzzled. Afrikaans: "verwar".

**belongings** – The things that belong to a person. e.g. Pack your belongings before the bus leaves. Afrikaans: "besittings".

**explanation** – A reason that makes something clear. e.g. The teacher gave a clear explanation. One explanation, many explanations. Afrikaans: "verklaring".

**logbook** – A book in which a captain writes down what happens on a ship each day. e.g. The captain wrote the weather in the logbook. Afrikaans: "logboek".

**evidence** – Facts or objects that help to show whether something is true. e.g. The muddy footprints were evidence that the dog had been inside. Afrikaans: "bewys".

*Extra cards for more support:* fumes, historian

### The passage

On 4 December 1872, the crew of a ship called the Dei Gratia spotted a strange sight in the Atlantic Ocean. A two-masted ship was drifting slowly across the waves, with nobody at the wheel. It was the Mary Celeste, and what they found on board has puzzled people for more than 150 years.

When sailors from the Dei Gratia climbed aboard, the ship was completely empty. There was no captain, no crew and no passengers. Yet the ship was in fairly good condition, and there was enough food and fresh water for six months. Many of the crew's belongings were still on board. The cargo of more than 1 700 barrels was almost untouched. The only lifeboat, however, was missing.

So what happened to the ten people on board? Over the years, people have suggested all sorts of explanations. Some blamed pirates, but nothing valuable had been stolen. Others imagined sea monsters or giant waves. Many stories were simply invented, including a famous one by the writer Arthur Conan Doyle, who later created the detective Sherlock Holmes.

Today, many historians believe the answer may be less dramatic. The captain's last logbook entry showed that the ship had been near the Azores islands. One theory is that fumes leaked from the barrels, and the captain feared an explosion. Another is that a pump was broken, and he thought the ship was sinking. Either way, he may have ordered everyone into the lifeboat to wait at a safe distance. If the lifeboat's rope snapped, the ship could have sailed away without them. Sadly, the lifeboat and its passengers were never seen again.

We may never know for certain what happened. That is what makes the Mary Celeste such a gripping mystery. It reminds us that even with clues, some questions have no final answer. Good detectives, like good historians, look carefully at the evidence and avoid jumping to conclusions. Which explanation do you think makes the most sense?

### Comprehension (5 questions, ✓ = correct)

1. **When was the Mary Celeste found drifting?** 4 July 1776. / **4 December 1872. ✓** / 1 January 1900. / 12 August 1782. / 25 December 1872.
2. **What was missing from the ship?** The food. / The water. / The cargo. / **The only lifeboat. ✓** / The sails.
3. **Why don't most people believe pirates were to blame?** Pirates never sailed there. / **Nothing valuable had been stolen. ✓** / The ship was too small. / Pirates only attack at night. / The crew fought them off.
4. **(Thinking) Why might the captain have ordered everyone into the lifeboat?** To go fishing. / To race the Dei Gratia. / **He feared an explosion or thought the ship was sinking. ✓** / To visit the Azores. / He saw a sea monster.
5. **(Thinking) What does the writer say good detectives and historians do?** They always know the answer. / They make up exciting stories. / They trust every rumour. / They blame pirates. / **They look carefully at the evidence and avoid jumping to conclusions. ✓**

### Word recognition (10)

strange, drifting, puzzled, condition, belongings, lifeboat, explanations, historians, logbook, evidence

### Grammar: the past perfect (5)

*Complete with had + the past participle – e.g. They \_\_\_ (leave). – They had left.*

1. When the sailors arrived, the crew \_\_\_ (disappear). – **had disappeared**
2. Nobody \_\_\_ (steal) the cargo. – **had stolen**
3. The captain \_\_\_ (write) in the logbook. – **had written**
4. The lifeboat \_\_\_ (go). – **had gone**
5. The crew \_\_\_ (leave) their boots behind. – **had left**

### Vocabulary (5)

1. The empty rowing boat \_\_\_\_\_\_ out to sea. drafted / **drifted ✓** / drifting / drilled
2. The strange message \_\_\_\_\_\_ everyone. puzzle / pedalled / **puzzled ✓** / muzzled
3. Pack all your \_\_\_\_\_\_ before we leave. **belongings ✓** / belonging / belongs / longings
4. Is there a sensible \_\_\_\_\_\_ for the noise? exploration / expectation / explanations / **explanation ✓**
5. The footprints were \_\_\_\_\_\_ that someone had been there. evident / evidences / event / **evidence ✓**

## Level 5 – English 9: Your Body's Defence Force

Topic: My body and health. About 350 words, 22 sentences, 5 paragraphs, informative.

### Word cards (6)

**immune system** – The parts of your body that fight germs and keep you healthy. e.g. Sleep and healthy food help your immune system. Afrikaans: "immuunstelsel".

**invader** – Someone or something that enters a place to attack it. e.g. The castle walls kept out invaders. One invader, many invaders. Afrikaans: "indringer".

**antibody** – A substance made by your body to fight a particular germ. e.g. Your body makes antibodies after you have had chickenpox. One antibody, many antibodies. Afrikaans: "teenliggaam".

**preview** – A chance to see something before the real thing happens. e.g. We watched a preview of the new film. Afrikaans: "voorskou".

**disease** – An illness. e.g. Measles is a disease that spreads easily. One disease, many diseases. Afrikaans: "siekte".

**community** – A group of people who live in the same area. e.g. Our community cleaned up the park. One community, many communities. Afrikaans: "gemeenskap".

*Extra cards for more support:* vaccinate, smallpox

### The passage

Every day, your body is under attack. Germs such as bacteria and viruses are all around us: in the air, on door handles and on our hands. Most of the time we don't even notice, because our bodies have an amazing defence force called the immune system.

The immune system is made up of special white blood cells that patrol your body, looking for invaders. When they find a germ, some of them make antibodies. Antibodies are a little like keys that fit onto one particular germ and help to destroy it. After the battle, some of these cells remember the germ. If the same germ attacks again, the body recognises it and fights back so fast that you may not get sick at all.

The problem is that the first time you meet a dangerous germ, your body needs time to learn how to fight it. During that time, you can become very ill. This is where vaccines help. A vaccine gives your immune system a safe preview of a germ, using a weakened or harmless version of it, or just a small piece of it. Your body practises making antibodies without you having to catch the real disease.

Vaccines have saved millions of lives. Smallpox, a disease that killed hundreds of millions of people over the centuries, has been completely wiped out thanks to vaccination. Polio, which once left many children unable to walk, has almost disappeared. When most people in a community are vaccinated, it is hard for a germ to spread, which also protects babies and people who are too sick to be vaccinated.

Like all medicines, vaccines are tested carefully before they are used, and doctors keep checking them afterwards. Some people feel a sore arm or a slight fever for a day or two, which is a sign that the immune system is doing its job. If you have questions about a vaccine, a nurse or doctor is the best person to ask. Your immune system is a remarkable team of defenders, and vaccines are simply a way of giving it a head start.

### Comprehension (5 questions, ✓ = correct)

1. **What is the immune system?** A kind of germ. / A medicine. / **The body's defence force against germs. ✓** / A part of the brain. / A type of food.
2. **What do antibodies do?** They make you sneeze. / They carry food around the body. / **They fit onto a particular germ and help to destroy it. ✓** / They make germs stronger. / They clean your teeth.
3. **How does a vaccine help your body?** It kills every germ in the world. / **It gives the immune system a safe preview, so it can practise without you catching the real disease. ✓** / It makes you stronger than other people. / It replaces your white blood cells. / It stops you from ever getting a cold.
4. **(Thinking) How can vaccinating most people in a community protect babies?** Babies get the vaccine through the air. / **When most people are vaccinated, it is hard for a germ to spread to anyone. ✓** / Babies don't have immune systems. / Germs don't like babies. / Doctors keep babies indoors.
5. **(Thinking) Why might a sore arm after a vaccine be a good sign?** It means the vaccine didn't work. / It means you are allergic. / **It shows that the immune system is doing its job. ✓** / It means you need another vaccine. / It shows the nurse made a mistake.

### Word recognition (10)

bacteria, viruses, defence, immune, antibodies, recognises, vaccine, weakened, smallpox, community

### Grammar: nouns ending in -ion (5)

*Make a noun – e.g. protect – protection*

1. vaccinate – **vaccination**
2. infect – **infection**
3. explain – **explanation**
4. decide – **decision**
5. collect – **collection**

### Vocabulary (5)

1. Healthy food helps your \_\_\_\_\_\_ system. immunity / **immune ✓** / image / immense
2. Your body makes \_\_\_\_\_\_ to fight germs. antibody / antiques / antelopes / **antibodies ✓**
3. Measles is a \_\_\_\_\_\_ that spreads easily. **disease ✓** / decease / diseases / discuss
4. The whole \_\_\_\_\_\_ helped to clean the park. comedy / compass / comment / **community ✓**
5. The castle walls kept out the \_\_\_\_\_\_. invaded / **invaders ✓** / investors / inventors

## Level 5 – English 10: Why Board Games Are Good for Your Brain

Topic: Sport and games. About 340 words, 24 sentences, 5 paragraphs, persuasive.

### Word cards (6)

**comeback** – A return to being popular or successful. e.g. Roller skates are making a comeback. Afrikaans: "terugkeer".

**in advance** – Before something happens. e.g. Book your tickets in advance. Afrikaans: "vooraf".

**opponent** – The person you play against in a game or match. e.g. My opponent was very clever. One opponent, many opponents. Afrikaans: "teenstander".

**gracefully** – In a calm, polite and pleasant way. e.g. She accepted the loss gracefully. Afrikaans: "met waardigheid".

**generation** – All the people born around the same time, such as your grandparents' generation. e.g. The recipe was passed down from generation to generation. One generation, many generations. Afrikaans: "geslag".

**variety** – A mix of many different things. e.g. The shop sells a variety of fruit. Afrikaans: "verskeidenheid".

*Extra cards for more support:* draughts, morabaraba

### The passage

In a world of video games and streaming, board games might seem old-fashioned. After all, people have been playing them for thousands of years. One of the oldest known board games, called Senet, was played in ancient Egypt more than 5 000 years ago. Yet board games are making a comeback, and scientists say that is good news for our brains.

Firstly, board games make us think ahead. In games like chess, draughts or morabaraba, you have to plan several moves in advance and imagine what your opponent might do. This kind of planning strengthens the parts of the brain we use for solving problems and making decisions. Games with dice and pretend money also give us plenty of practice with mental maths.

Secondly, board games teach us how to win and lose gracefully. When you land on the wrong square or your plan fails, you have to stay calm and try again. Learning to congratulate the winner, even when you are disappointed, is an important life skill. Many games also require teamwork, patience and waiting for your turn, which are not always easy!

Thirdly, board games bring people together. Unlike many screen games, they are played face to face. Families and friends sit around the same table, laugh, argue and chat. Grandparents and grandchildren can play together, even if they have very different interests. Morabaraba, for example, has been passed down through many generations in Southern Africa, often scratched into the ground or carved into rock.

Of course, video games can also be fun and can even teach useful skills. The point is not that one is good and the other is bad, but that variety is healthy for our minds. So, the next time it is raining or the power goes off, dig out an old board game instead of sighing with boredom. You might discover that the best game in the house has been sitting in the cupboard all along. You may also find yourself thinking more sharply, laughing more loudly and getting to know your family a little better.

### Comprehension (5 questions, ✓ = correct)

1. **Where was the ancient game Senet played?** In China. / In Greece. / **In ancient Egypt. ✓** / In South Africa. / In Rome.
2. **How do games like chess and morabaraba help the brain?** They make us sleepy. / **They make us plan ahead, which helps with solving problems and making decisions. ✓** / They make us draw better. / They help us read faster. / They teach us to sing.
3. **What life skill does losing a board game teach?** How to cheat. / How to shout. / How to give up. / **How to stay calm and congratulate the winner. ✓** / How to hide the pieces.
4. **(Thinking) Why does the writer mention grandparents and grandchildren?** Because only old people play board games. / **To show that board games bring different generations together. ✓** / Because grandparents always win. / Because grandchildren need help. / To show that games are for children.
5. **(Thinking) Is the writer against video games?** Yes, the writer thinks they are bad. / Yes, the writer wants them banned. / **No, the writer says variety is healthy for our minds. ✓** / No, the writer only plays video games. / The writer never mentions them.

### Word recognition (10)

old-fashioned, ancient, comeback, draughts, morabaraba, opponent, strengthens, gracefully, congratulate, generations

1. loudly – **more loudly**

5. badly – **worse**
