### English 1: The School Play

Our class is putting on a play about a lion and a mouse.\
I am the mouse, and my friend Sam is the lion.\
We practise our lines every day after lunch.\
On the night of the play, the hall is full of parents.\
My hands are shaking, and my tummy feels funny.\
When the curtain opens, I see my mom in the front row.\
She gives me a big thumbs up.\
I say all my lines without a single mistake.\
Sam roars so loudly that a baby starts to cry!\
At the end, everyone claps and cheers.\
We bow three times.\
I can't wait for next year's play.

1. **What is the play about?** A cat and a dog. / **A lion and a mouse. ✓** / A king and a queen. / A frog and a bird. / A fox and a crow.

3. **Where is Mom sitting?** At the back. / On the stage. / Outside. / **In the front row. ✓** / Next to Sam.
4. **What happens when Sam roars?** The curtain falls. / The lights go out. / I forget my lines. / Everyone leaves. / **A baby starts to cry. ✓**

**Spelling (7):** practise, parents, shaking, curtain, mistake, roars, cheers

2. **Where do the classes line up?** In the hall. / In the car park. / At the gate. / **At the big tree next to the sports field. ✓** / In the classroom.

4. **How long does it take them to get outside?** One minute. / **Three minutes. ✓** / Five minutes. / Ten minutes. / Half an hour.

**Spelling (7):** middle, drill, calmly, straight, allowed, principal, stopwatch

### English 1: The Wobbly Tooth

Lindiwe's front tooth had been wobbly for almost two weeks.\
She wiggled it with her tongue during lessons and at bedtime.\
Her big brother offered to tie it to a door with string, but she said no!\
At lunchtime on Wednesday, she bit into a crunchy green apple.\
Suddenly, she felt something hard in her mouth.\
It was her tooth!\
There was a little gap in her smile, and she could whistle through it.\
Her teacher gave her a tiny envelope to keep the tooth safe.\
That night, Lindiwe put the envelope under her pillow.\
She tried to stay awake to see the tooth mouse, but her eyes kept closing.\
In the morning, she lifted her pillow with a pounding heart.\
The envelope was gone.\
In its place were two shiny coins and a note in very small writing.\
"Thank you for the beautiful tooth. Keep brushing! Love, the Tooth Mouse."\
Lindiwe smiled her gappy smile all the way to school.

**Comprehension (4 questions, ✓ = correct)**

1. **How long had the tooth been wobbly?** Two days. / **Almost two weeks. ✓** / A month. / A year. / Since Monday.
2. **What did Lindiwe bite into?** A hard biscuit. / A sandwich. / **A crunchy green apple. ✓** / A carrot. / A toffee.
3. **What did her teacher give her?** A plaster. / A sticker. / A coin. / **A tiny envelope. ✓** / A box.
4. **What was under the pillow in the morning?** Nothing. / The tooth. / A toothbrush. / A sweet. / **Two shiny coins and a note. ✓**

**Spelling (7):** wobbly, wiggled, crunchy, whistle, envelope, pillow, pounding

### English 2: Moving House

Ethan was not happy when his family moved from Johannesburg to a small town in the Free State.\
He had to leave his school, his soccer team and his best friend, Kyle.\
On the long drive, he stared out of the window at the flat, yellow fields.\
Their new house was old, with a creaky wooden floor and a big pepper tree in the yard.\
Ethan's bedroom was painted a boring grey.\
"We can paint it any colour you like," said his mom.\
On the first Saturday, a boy about Ethan's age knocked on the door.\
He was holding a soccer ball under his arm.\
"I'm Thabo from next door," he said. "Do you want to play?"\
They kicked the ball in the dusty street until the sun went down.\
Thabo told him about the river where they could swim and the dam full of frogs.\
That evening, Ethan chose a bright blue paint for his room.\
Maybe this small town wouldn't be so bad after all.

**Comprehension (4 questions, ✓ = correct)**

### English 3: The Spelling Bee

Every year, Riverside Primary holds a spelling bee for the Grade 4 classes.\
This year, Nomvula was chosen to represent her class.\
For three weeks, she practised with her gran every evening after supper.\
Gran read words from a long list, and Nomvula spelt them out loud.\
On the day of the competition, twelve children stood on the stage in the hall.\
One by one, they were given words to spell.\
Some children got nervous and made small mistakes.\
Soon only Nomvula and a tall boy named Pieter were left.\
Pieter was given the word "rhythm".\
He spelt it without the second "h", and the judge shook her head.\
Now Nomvula had to spell it correctly to win.\
Her mouth felt dry, but she remembered Gran's trick for tricky words.\
She pictured the word written on the kitchen table in big letters.\
"R-H-Y-T-H-M," she said slowly.\
The hall burst into applause.\
Nomvula ran straight to Gran, who was sitting in the front row, clapping the loudest of all.

**Comprehension (4 questions, ✓ = correct)**

2. **How many children stood on the stage?** Six. / Ten. / **Twelve. ✓** / Twenty. / Four.
3. **Which word did Pieter spell wrongly?** Rhyme. / **Rhythm. ✓** / Rhino. / Receive. / Riddle.
4. **What was Gran's trick for tricky words?** Say them ten times. / Sing them as a song. / **Picture the word written on the kitchen table in big letters. ✓** / Write them on your hand. / Close your eyes and guess.

### Afrikaans 1: Koko het weggeloop

Mev. Botha se grys papegaai, Koko, is die slimste voël in die straat. Hy kan fluit soos 'n ketel, lag soos 'n kind en selfs die telefoon naboots. Een warm middag los Mev. Botha per ongeluk die voordeur oop terwyl sy die wasgoed afhaal.

Toe sy terugkom, is Koko se hok leeg. Sy soek onder die banke, agter die gordyne en selfs in die kombuiskaste, maar Koko is weg. Huilend klop sy aan die bure se deure en vra of iemand 'n grys voël met 'n rooi stert gesien het.

Die kinders in die straat besluit om te help. Ruan maak plakkate met 'n tekening van Koko en plak dit teen die lamppale. Zinhle ry op haar fiets van straat tot straat en roep sy naam. Die kleintjies soek in die park en tussen die bome by die rivier. Teen sononder het niemand hom nog gesien nie, en almal gaan teleurgesteld huis toe.

Die volgende oggend lui Mev. Botha se telefoon. Dit is die dame van die bakkery twee strate verder. "Ek dink ek het u voël," lag sy. "Hy sit bo-op my toonbank, eet 'n koekie en fluit vir al my kliënte!" Mev. Botha haas haar met Koko se hok na die bakkery.

Sodra Koko haar sien, roep hy: "Hallo, Mamma!" en vlieg reguit op haar skouer. Die hele bakkery klap hande. Om die kinders te bedank, bak die bakkery-dame 'n groot koek in die vorm van 'n papegaai. Van toe af is Mev. Botha baie versigtig om die deur toe te hou.

### Afrikaans 2: Die geheim van die potlood

Byna elke kind in Suid-Afrika het 'n potlood in sy skooltas. Ons gebruik dit om te skryf, te teken en somme te maak. Maar het jy al ooit gewonder hoe die donker "lood" binne-in die hout kom?

Die eerste verrassing is dat daar glad nie lood in 'n potlood is nie! Die donker deel word van grafiet gemaak, 'n sagte, gryserige mineraal wat uit die grond gemyn word. Lank gelede het mense gedink grafiet is 'n soort lood, en die naam het net bly vassteek.

In die fabriek word die grafiet fyngemaal en met klei en water gemeng. Hoe meer klei by die mengsel gevoeg word, hoe harder en ligter is die potlood se punt. Die mengsel word in lang, dun stafies gedruk en in 'n baie warm oond gebak.

Intussen word hout, dikwels van seder- of dennebome, in dun plankies gesaag. Masjiene sny klein groefies in die plankies. Die grafietstafies word in die groefies gelê, en 'n tweede plankie word bo-op vasgeplak, soos 'n toebroodjie. Daarna word die "toebroodjie" in afsonderlike potlode gesny en glad geskaaf. Laastens word die potlode geverf, en party kry 'n uitveër aan die een kant.

'n Enkele potlood kan 'n lyn van omtrent vyftig kilometer trek voordat dit opgebruik is! Dit kan ook onderstebo skryf en is selfs al in die ruimte gebruik. Die volgende keer wat jy jou potlood skerp maak, dink aan die lang reis wat dit afgelê het: van die myn na die fabriek, en uiteindelik na jou klaskamer.

**Begrip (4 vrae, ✓ = reg)**

1. **Waarvan word die donker deel van 'n potlood gemaak?** Van lood. / Van steenkool. / **Van grafiet. ✓** / Van ink. / Van klip.
2. **Waarmee word die grafiet gemeng?** Met olie. / **Met klei en water. ✓** / Met sand. / Met verf. / Met gom.
3. **Waarmee vergelyk die leesstuk die twee plankies met die grafiet tussenin?** Met 'n boek. / Met 'n brug. / Met 'n koek. / **Met 'n toebroodjie. ✓** / Met 'n doos.
4. **Hoe lank is die lyn wat 'n enkele potlood omtrent kan trek?** Een kilometer. / Tien meter. / Honderd kilometer. / Vyf kilometer. / **Vyftig kilometer. ✓**

**Spelling (7):** potlood, grafiet, mineraal, fyngemaal, stafies, groefies, uitveër

### Afrikaans 3: Toe Oupa klein was

Toe Lwazi 'n skoolprojek oor "vroeër jare" moes doen, het hy sy oupa gaan besoek. Oupa Joseph is tagtig jaar oud en woon op 'n klein plaas naby Queenstown. Hy het sy kierie neergesit, in sy ou leunstoel gaan sit en begin vertel.

"Toe ek so oud soos jy was, het ons nie elektrisiteit gehad nie," het Oupa gesê. "Saans het ons by die lig van 'n paraffienlamp huiswerk gedoen." Daar was geen televisie of selfone nie. Die kinders het self hul speelgoed gemaak, soos karretjies van draad en balle van ou lappe.

Oupa het elke dag tien kilometer skool toe gestap, kaalvoet, selfs in die winter. Sy klas was in 'n klein gebou met net een lokaal, en al die grade het saam gesit. Die kinders het op klein swart leitjies geskryf, want papier was duur. Na skool moes hy die koeie oppas en water by die fontein gaan haal.

Lwazi was verbaas. "Was dit nie baie moeilik nie?" het hy gevra. Oupa het gelag. "Dit was moeilik, maar ons was gelukkig. Ons het buite gespeel tot die son ondergaan, en ons het mekaar gehelp." Hy het vertel hoe die hele dorpie saamgewerk het om mekaar se huise te bou en die oes in te samel.

Voor Lwazi gery het, het Oupa 'n ou draadkarretjie uit 'n kas gehaal. "Ek het dit gemaak toe ek tien was," het hy gesê. Lwazi het die karretjie saamgeneem skool toe vir sy projek. Sy klasmaats kon nie glo dat iemand so iets met sy hande kon maak nie. Daardie aand het Lwazi sy foon neergesit en saam met sy pa sy eie draadkarretjie begin bou.

**Begrip (4 vrae, ✓ = reg)**

1. **Hoe oud is Oupa Joseph?** Sestig. / Sewentig. / **Tagtig. ✓** / Negentig. / Honderd.
2. **Waarby het Oupa saans huiswerk gedoen?** By die vuur. / By 'n kers. / By die maanlig. / **By die lig van 'n paraffienlamp. ✓** / By 'n gloeilamp.
3. **Hoe ver het Oupa elke dag skool toe gestap?** Een kilometer. / Twee kilometer. / **Tien kilometer. ✓** / Twintig kilometer. / Hy het met die bus gery.
4. **Wat het Oupa vir Lwazi gegee?** 'n Ou leitjie. / 'n Paraffienlamp. / 'n Bal van lappe. / Sy kierie. / **'n Ou draadkarretjie. ✓**

**Spelling (7):** projek, leunstoel, elektrisiteit, paraffienlamp, kaalvoet, fontein, draadkarretjie

### English 1: The Lunchbox Thief

For a whole week, something strange had been happening at Oakwood Primary. Every day at break, somebody's lunch went missing from the Grade 5 classroom. On Monday, it was Aisha's cheese sandwich. On Tuesday, Ben's banana disappeared, and on Wednesday, Thandi's muffin vanished without a trace.

The class decided to become detectives. Leo drew a map of the classroom, and Aisha made a list of everything that had gone missing. They noticed something interesting: the thief only took food, never pencils or money. And the lunches always disappeared from bags near the open window.

On Thursday, they set a trap. Ben left a peanut butter sandwich on top of his bag, right next to the window. Then the whole class hid behind the door and waited in silence. After ten minutes, they heard a soft scratching sound. Something grey and furry climbed through the window!

It was a monkey. It grabbed the sandwich with its little hands, looked straight at the class and chattered loudly. Then it leapt back out of the window and scampered up the big fig tree. The children burst out laughing. The famous lunch thief was a hungry vervet monkey!

Their teacher explained that monkeys often come into town looking for easy food. Feeding them, even by accident, teaches them to keep coming back. From then on, the class kept the window closed during break and put their lunches in a cupboard. The monkey still sat in the fig tree every day, watching hopefully. But it never got another sandwich.

**Comprehension (4 questions, ✓ = correct)**

1. **Which classroom were the lunches taken from?** Grade 3. / Grade 4. / **Grade 5. ✓** / Grade 6. / Grade 7.
2. **Where were the bags that the lunches disappeared from?** Under the desks. / In the cupboard. / Near the door. / **Near the open window. ✓** / In the hall.
3. **What did Ben leave as a trap?** A cheese sandwich. / A banana. / A muffin. / **A peanut butter sandwich. ✓** / An apple.
4. **Where did the monkey go with the sandwich?** Onto the roof. / Into the hall. / Under a car. / Over the fence. / **Up the big fig tree. ✓**

**Spelling (7):** strange, classroom, detectives, noticed, window, scratching, scampered

### English 2: Up, Up and Away

Early one morning, Ruby and her parents drove to a wide, open field in the Magaliesberg. They had come to take a ride in a hot-air balloon. In the grey light, the huge, colourful balloon lay flat on the grass like a sleeping giant.

First, the crew used a big fan to blow cold air into the balloon. Then the pilot turned on the burner, which roared like a dragon. A huge flame shot upwards and heated the air inside. Slowly, the balloon rose from the ground and stood upright.

"Hot air is lighter than cold air," explained the pilot. "When the air inside the balloon is hotter than the air outside, the balloon floats upwards, just like a bubble rising in water." To go higher, he turned on the burner. To go lower, he let the air cool down. "But we can't steer," he said. "We go wherever the wind takes us."

Ruby held on tightly as the basket lifted off the ground. It was surprisingly quiet and smooth. Below them, the fields looked like a patchwork quilt, and the cows looked as small as ants. They floated over a river, where a heron was fishing, and waved at a farmer on his tractor.

After an hour, the pilot let the balloon sink gently into a farmer's field. A truck followed them to carry the balloon and the basket back. To celebrate the flight, the crew gave everyone a glass of fruit juice and a certificate. Ruby knew she would remember this morning forever.

**Comprehension (4 questions, ✓ = correct)**

1. **Where did Ruby go for the balloon ride?** To a beach in Durban. / **To a field in the Magaliesberg. ✓** / To a farm in the Karoo. / To a park in Cape Town. / To the top of a mountain.
2. **What did the crew use first to fill the balloon?** The burner. / A pump. / **A big fan blowing cold air. ✓** / Helium gas. / Their breath.
3. **How does the pilot make the balloon go lower?** He opens the basket. / He throws out sandbags. / **He lets the air cool down. ✓** / He turns on the fan. / He pulls a rope to the ground.
4. **What did the cows look like from above?** As big as elephants. / Like clouds. / Like toys in a box. / Like dots of paint. / **As small as ants. ✓**

**Spelling (7):** balloon, colourful, burner, upwards, explained, steer, patchwork

### English 3: The Library Sleepover

Every year, the Sunnyside Library holds a sleepover for readers who have finished twenty books. This year, for the first time, Kagiso had reached the target. On Friday evening, he arrived with his sleeping bag, pillow and torch.

Twenty children spread their sleeping bags between the tall bookshelves. The librarian, Mr Khumalo, turned off the main lights. "Tonight we are going on a treasure hunt," he whispered. He handed each pair of children a card with a riddle on it. The answer to each riddle was the title of a book hidden somewhere in the library.

Kagiso and his partner, Zoe, read their first riddle by torchlight. "I am a big grey animal who never forgets," it said. They searched the animal section and found a book about elephants. Inside it was the next clue! The riddles led them to the cookery shelf, the atlas and finally the poetry corner.

At the end of the hunt, they found a golden envelope inside a book of fairy tales. It contained a voucher for a new book of their choice. Everyone cheered so loudly that Mr Khumalo had to remind them it was nearly midnight.

Before going to sleep, the children took turns reading their favourite stories aloud by torchlight. Kagiso read a funny story about a crocodile who wanted to be a dentist. One by one, the torches clicked off. Kagiso lay in his sleeping bag, surrounded by thousands of books, and smiled. He had already decided that next year, he would read forty books.

**Comprehension (4 questions, ✓ = correct)**

1. **How many books did readers need to finish to be invited?** Ten. / **Twenty. ✓** / Thirty. / Forty. / Fifty.
2. **What was the answer to each riddle?** A number. / An animal. / **The title of a book hidden in the library. ✓** / A person's name. / A place in the town.
3. **Which animal was the first riddle about?** A lion. / A crocodile. / A whale. / **An elephant. ✓** / A rhino.
4. **What was inside the golden envelope?** A key. / A map. / Chocolate coins. / A letter from an author. / **A voucher for a new book. ✓**

**Spelling (7):** library, sleepover, bookshelves, librarian, riddle, torchlight, voucher

### Afrikaans 1: Lees is jou superkrag

In 'n wêreld vol skerms, video's en speletjies vra baie kinders: "Hoekom moet ek nog boeke lees?" Dit is 'n goeie vraag. Die antwoord is eenvoudig, maar verrassend: om gereeld te lees, is een van die kragtigste dinge wat jy vir jou brein kan doen.

Wanneer jy lees, werk jou brein harder as wanneer jy na 'n video kyk. Jy moet die woorde ontsyfer, prentjies in jou kop skep en onthou wat vroeër in die storie gebeur het. Navorsers het gevind dat kinders wat elke dag lees, beter konsentreer en 'n groter woordeskat het. Met elke boek leer jy honderde nuwe woorde sonder dat jy dit eers agterkom.

Lees help jou ook in elke skoolvak. Om 'n wiskundeprobleem op te los, moet jy eers die vraag verstaan. In wetenskap, geskiedenis en geografie lees jy om nuwe inligting te leer. Kinders wat vlot lees, vind dus dikwels al hul vakke makliker. Hulle skryf ook beter, want hulle het gesien hoe goeie skrywers sinne bou.

Maar lees is nie net vir punte nie. 'n Boek kan jou na plekke neem wat jy nog nooit gesien het nie: die diepte van die see, 'n kasteel in die wolke of 'n dorpie aan die ander kant van die wêreld. Wanneer jy lees oor karakters wat anders is as jy, leer jy om te verstaan hoe ander mense voel. Wetenskaplikes noem dit empatie. Lees kan jou ook help om te ontspan en rustig te raak na 'n besige dag.

Hoe word jy 'n leser? Begin met boeke oor dinge waarvan jy hou, of dit nou dinosourusse, sokker of towerstories is. Lees elke dag, al is dit net tien minute voor jy gaan slaap. Besoek die biblioteek, ruil boeke met jou maats, of lees saam met 'n boetie of sussie. Moenie bekommerd wees as jy stadig lees nie; hoe meer jy oefen, hoe vinniger en makliker word dit. Jy hoef nie 'n kapmantel te dra om 'n superheld te wees nie. Soms is 'n goeie boek al die superkrag wat jy nodig het.

**Begrip (4 vrae, ✓ = reg)**

1. **Wat het navorsers gevind oor kinders wat elke dag lees?** Hulle slaap langer. / Hulle word vinniger hardlopers. / **Hulle konsentreer beter en het 'n groter woordeskat. ✓** / Hulle kyk minder TV. / Hulle het meer vriende.
2. **Waarom help lees ook in wiskunde?** Omdat somme in boeke staan. / **Omdat jy eers die vraag moet verstaan om 'n probleem op te los. ✓** / Omdat getalle woorde is. / Omdat jy minder hoef te tel. / Omdat onderwysers dit sê.
3. **Wat noem wetenskaplikes dit wanneer jy leer verstaan hoe ander mense voel?** Konsentrasie. / Woordeskat. / Verbeelding. / **Empatie. ✓** / Superkrag.
4. **Hoe lank moet jy volgens die skrywer ten minste elke dag lees?** 'n Uur. / 'n Halfuur. / Vyf minute. / Twee uur. / **Tien minute. ✓**

**Spelling (7):** ontsyfer, navorsers, woordeskat, wiskundeprobleem, geskiedenis, empatie, biblioteek

### Afrikaans 2: Die Groot Migrasie

Elke jaar vind een van die grootste natuurwonders op aarde in Oos-Afrika plaas. Meer as een miljoen wildebeeste, saam met honderdduisende sebras en gazelles, trek in 'n reuse sirkel deur Tanzanië en Kenia. Die troppe is so groot dat 'n mens hulle selfs van 'n vliegtuig af kan sien. Hierdie reis word die Groot Migrasie genoem.

Die diere volg die reën. Wanneer die grasvlaktes van die Serengeti in Tanzanië opdroog, trek die troppe noord op soek na vars gras. Hulle reis honderde kilometers en bereik teen die middel van die jaar die Masai Mara in Kenia. Wanneer die reën later weer in die suide val, keer hulle terug. Die hele reis duur 'n jaar en herhaal elke jaar.

Die gevaarlikste deel van die reis is die oorsteek van die Mararivier. Die water vloei vinnig, en die walle is steil en glad. In die rivier wag groot krokodille, en op die oewer lê leeus en ander roofdiere en wag. Duisende wildebeeste huiwer by die water, totdat een dapper dier spring en die res blindelings volg. Ongelukkig haal nie almal die oorkant nie.

Tog is die migrasie 'n wonderlike voorbeeld van hoe die natuur in balans bly. Die groot troppe bemes die grond, wat nuwe gras laat groei. Die karkasse van diere wat sterf, voed aasvoëls, hiënas en selfs visse in die rivier. Omtrent 'n halwe miljoen kalwers word elke jaar gebore, gewoonlik binne 'n paar weke van mekaar. Omdat daar so baie gelyk gebore word, kan roofdiere nie almal vang nie.

Vandag besoek toeriste van oor die hele wêreld die gebied om hierdie skouspel met hul eie oë te sien. Maar die migrasie is kwesbaar. Heinings, paaie en plase kan die diere se roete versper, en droogtes word erger. Natuurbewaarders werk hard om die groot vlaktes oop te hou, sodat die diere vry kan beweeg. As hulle daarin slaag, sal ons kleinkinders eendag ook kan sien hoe die aarde bewe onder miljoene hoewe. Dit is 'n skouspel wat die moeite werd is om te beskerm.

**Begrip (4 vrae, ✓ = reg)**

1. **Hoeveel wildebeeste neem aan die migrasie deel?** Omtrent tienduisend. / Honderd. / **Meer as een miljoen. ✓** / Vyftig duisend. / Tien miljoen.
2. **Waarom trek die troppe noord?** Om van die leeus weg te kom. / Omdat dit koud word. / **Hulle volg die reën op soek na vars gras. ✓** / Om te swem. / Om hul kalwers te versteek.
3. **Watter deel van die reis is die gevaarlikste?** Die reis deur die woestyn. / **Die oorsteek van die Mararivier. ✓** / Die klim teen die berge. / Die reis by die see. / Die tyd in die Serengeti.
4. **Hoeveel kalwers word omtrent elke jaar gebore?** Duisend. / Tienduisend. / Een miljoen. / Honderd. / **'n Halwe miljoen. ✓**

**Spelling (7):** wildebeeste, migrasie, grasvlaktes, krokodille, roofdiere, karkasse, natuurbewaarders

### Afrikaans 3: Van skaduwee tot atoomhorlosie

Vandag kyk ons net na ons foon of horlosie om te weet hoe laat dit is. Maar vir duisende jare het mense geen horlosies gehad nie. Tog het hulle slim maniere gevind om die tyd te meet.

Die eerste "horlosie" was die son self. Mense het opgelet dat skaduwees gedurende die dag van rigting en lengte verander. Die ou Egiptenare het meer as drieduisend jaar gelede sonwysers gebou. 'n Sonwyser het 'n regop pen wat 'n skaduwee op 'n plat skyf met merke gooi. Soos die son oor die lug beweeg, skuif die skaduwee van merk tot merk. Die probleem was natuurlik dat sonwysers snags of op bewolkte dae nutteloos was.

Daarom het mense ander uitvindings gemaak. 'n Waterhorlosie was 'n bak met 'n klein gaatjie onder in. Die water het stadig uitgedrup, en merke aan die kant het gewys hoeveel tyd verby is. Later het mense uurglase gebruik, waarin fyn sand van een glas na 'n ander deur 'n smal nek loop. In party lande het mense selfs kerse met merke laat brand om die ure te tel.

Die eerste meganiese horlosies is omtrent sewehonderd jaar gelede in Europa gebou. Hulle was so groot dat hulle in kerktorings geplaas is, en baie het nie eens 'n minuutwyser gehad nie. Mettertyd het horlosiemakers hulle kleiner en akkurater gemaak, totdat mense hulle in hul sakke kon dra. Vandag meet atoomhorlosies die tyd so akkuraat dat hulle in miljoene jare skaars een sekonde fouteer. Hulle help selfs die GPS in ons fone om ons presies te vind.

Dit is verbasend om te dink hoe ver ons gekom het, van 'n stok in die sand tot 'n horlosie wat die trillings van atome tel. Tog is die basiese idee nog dieselfde: ons soek iets wat op 'n gereelde manier verander, en ons tel dit. Die volgende keer wat jy vra hoe laat dit is, onthou die slim uitvinders wat dit vir ons moontlik gemaak het. Dalk sal jy eendag self die volgende groot uitvinding maak!

**Begrip (4 vrae, ✓ = reg)**

1. **Wie het meer as drieduisend jaar gelede sonwysers gebou?** Die Romeine. / Die Chinese. / **Die ou Egiptenare. ✓** / Die Grieke. / Die Europeërs.
2. **Wanneer was sonwysers nutteloos?** In die somer. / Teen middagete. / **Snags of op bewolkte dae. ✓** / Wanneer dit waai. / In die oggend.
3. **Hoe het 'n waterhorlosie gewerk?** Water is in 'n pot gekook. / 'n Wiel het in die water gedraai. / **Water het stadig deur 'n gaatjie uitgedrup, en merke het die tyd gewys. ✓** / Die water het gevries. / Iemand het die druppels getel.
4. **Waar is die eerste meganiese horlosies geplaas?** In mense se sakke. / Op tafels. / By die see. / **In kerktorings. ✓** / In skole.

**Spelling (7):** horlosie, skaduwees, Egiptenare, sonwysers, uitvindings, uurglase, akkurater

### English 1: Speak a New Language, See a New World

South Africa has twelve official languages, including South African Sign Language. Yet many of us can speak only one or two of them. In my opinion, every child should learn at least one more language, and the earlier, the better.

Firstly, learning a language is excellent exercise for the brain. When you switch between languages, your brain has to decide which words to use and which to ignore. Researchers have found that people who speak more than one language are often good at solving problems and paying attention. Children's brains are especially good at picking up new sounds, which is why young learners often develop better accents.

Secondly, languages open doors to friendship. Imagine greeting a new classmate in their home language: "Sawubona", "Dumela" or "Molo". Their face would light up! When we make the effort to speak someone else's language, we show them respect. In a country with so many cultures, this can help to build understanding between people.

Thirdly, knowing more languages can help you in the future. Many jobs, especially in tourism, medicine and business, need people who can speak to others in their own language. A nurse who can speak isiZulu, Afrikaans and English, for example, can comfort many more patients. Languages also make travelling more fun, because you can chat to local people, read menus and understand signs.

Some people argue that learning a new language is too difficult or takes too much time. It is true that it requires patience, and you will make mistakes. But nobody expects you to be perfect at the start. Learn a few words every day, listen to songs and watch programmes in the new language, and practise with friends who speak it. Little by little, the words will start to make sense. A famous saying tells us that a different language is a different way of seeing life. Why not start seeing the world in a new way today?

**Comprehension (4 questions, ✓ = correct)**

1. **How many official languages does South Africa have?** Two. / Nine. / Eleven. / **Twelve. ✓** / Twenty.
2. **Why do young learners often develop better accents?** They have more time. / Their teachers are better. / **Children's brains are especially good at picking up new sounds. ✓** / They watch more TV. / They are not shy.
3. **What do we show someone when we make the effort to speak their language?** That we are clever. / That we are rich. / **That we respect them. ✓** / That we want something. / That we are bored.
4. **Which job does the writer use as an example?** A pilot. / A teacher. / A farmer. / **A nurse. ✓** / A chef.

**Spelling (7):** official, languages, exercise, researchers, accents, greeting, tourism

### English 2: The Songs of the Humpback Whale

Every winter, thousands of humpback whales swim past the coast of South Africa. They travel from the icy waters of Antarctica, where they feed in summer, to warmer waters further north, where they give birth. Their journey is one of the longest migrations of any mammal on Earth.

Humpback whales are enormous. An adult can be as long as a bus and weigh as much as several elephants. Yet they feed on some of the smallest animals in the sea: tiny, shrimp-like creatures called krill. To catch them, a group of whales sometimes swims in a circle below a shoal, blowing bubbles to form a "net". Then they rise through the middle with their mouths wide open.

The most amazing thing about humpbacks, however, is their singing. Male humpbacks sing long, complex songs made of moans, squeaks and deep rumbles. A single song can last for up to twenty minutes, and a whale may repeat it for hours. The sound can travel for kilometres through the water. Scientists believe the songs may help males to attract females, but nobody knows for certain.

Even more remarkably, all the males in one area sing the same song. Over time, the song slowly changes, and every whale changes it in the same way. Sometimes a completely new song spreads from one ocean region to another, like a hit song on the radio. This shows that whales can learn from one another, which is very rare in the animal world.

Not long ago, humpback whales were in serious trouble. Hunters killed so many that they almost disappeared. After whaling was banned, their numbers slowly grew again. Today, you can often see them from the shore or on a boat trip along the coasts of KwaZulu-Natal and the Western Cape. If you are lucky, you might even see one leap right out of the water. And if you could put your head underwater, you might just hear one singing.

**Comprehension (4 questions, ✓ = correct)**

1. **Where do humpback whales feed in summer?** Along the coast of KwaZulu-Natal. / **In the icy waters of Antarctica. ✓** / In warm waters near the equator. / In rivers. / Near Madagascar.
2. **What are krill?** Small fish. / Seaweed. / A type of whale. / **Tiny, shrimp-like creatures. ✓** / Jellyfish.
3. **How long can a single whale song last?** One minute. / **Up to twenty minutes. ✓** / A whole day. / Ten seconds. / Two hours.
4. **What happened after whaling was banned?** The whales moved away. / The whales stopped singing. / **Their numbers slowly grew again. ✓** / They became smaller. / Nothing changed.

**Spelling (7):** humpback, Antarctica, migrations, enormous, krill, complex, whaling

### English 3: The Race to the South Pole

In 1911, two teams of explorers set out on a dangerous race. Both wanted to be the first people ever to reach the South Pole, the coldest and loneliest place on Earth. One team was led by Roald Amundsen from Norway. The other was led by Robert Falcon Scott from Britain.

The two leaders planned their journeys very differently. Amundsen had learnt from the Inuit people of the Arctic. His team wore warm clothes made of animal fur and travelled on skis, with sledges pulled by dogs. Scott relied partly on motor sledges and ponies, but the engines broke down in the cold, and the ponies struggled in the snow. In the end, Scott's men had to pull their heavy sledges themselves.

Amundsen's team moved quickly across the ice. On 14 December 1911, they reached the South Pole and planted the Norwegian flag. They left a tent there, with a letter for Scott inside. Then they turned around and made it safely back to their base.

Scott's team arrived at the Pole about five weeks later, on 17 January 1912. When they saw the Norwegian flag flapping in the wind, they were heartbroken. The journey back was terrible. Blizzards raged, food ran short and temperatures dropped below minus forty degrees Celsius. Sadly, Scott and his four companions never made it home.

Today, both men are remembered for their courage. Scott's diaries, found months later, showed how bravely his team had faced their fate. Amundsen's success showed the value of careful planning and of learning from people who already know how to survive in harsh places. A research station now stands at the South Pole, named after both explorers: the Amundsen–Scott South Pole Station. The race is long over, but its lessons about preparation, teamwork and bravery are still important today.

**Comprehension (4 questions, ✓ = correct)**

1. **Which country was Amundsen from?** Britain. / Sweden. / **Norway. ✓** / Canada. / South Africa.
2. **What pulled the sledges of Amundsen's team?** Ponies. / Motor sledges. / The men themselves. / **Dogs. ✓** / Reindeer.
3. **When did Amundsen's team reach the South Pole?** 17 January 1912. / **14 December 1911. ✓** / 1 January 1911. / 25 December 1912. / 14 February 1911.
4. **What is the research station at the South Pole called?** Scott Station. / Norway Station. / Polar Base One. / Amundsen Camp. / **The Amundsen–Scott South Pole Station. ✓**

**Spelling (7):** explorers, dangerous, loneliest, differently, sledges, blizzards, temperatures
